# Switzerland

Switzerland

Country in Central Europe

This article is about the country. For other uses, see [Switzerland (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland_(disambiguation) "Switzerland (disambiguation)").

"Swiss Confederation" redirects here. For other uses, see [Swiss Confederation (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Confederation_(disambiguation) "Swiss Confederation (disambiguation)").

**Switzerland**,[\[d\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-18) officially the **Swiss Confederation**,[\[e\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-19) is a [landlocked country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landlocked_country "Landlocked country")[\[15\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-20) located at the intersection of [Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Europe "Central Europe"), [Western](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Europe "Western Europe"), and [Southern Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Europe "Southern Europe").[\[f\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-CH_definition-21)[\[g\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-24)[\[18\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-25) It is bordered by [Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germany "Germany") to the north, [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/France "France") to the west, [Austria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austria "Austria") and [Liechtenstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liechtenstein "Liechtenstein") to the east, and [Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italy "Italy") to the south. Switzerland is geographically divided among the [Swiss Alps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Alps "Swiss Alps"), the [Swiss Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Plateau "Swiss Plateau"), and the [Jura Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jura_Mountains "Jura Mountains"); the Alps cover most of the country's territory, whereas the majority of its [9 million people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Switzerland "Demographics of Switzerland") are concentrated on the plateau, which hosts many of the [largest cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_cities_in_Switzerland "List of cities in Switzerland") and economic centres, including [Zurich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zurich "Zurich"), [Geneva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva "Geneva"), [Basel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basel "Basel"), [Bern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bern "Bern"), [Lausanne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lausanne "Lausanne"), [Winterthur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winterthur "Winterthur"), and [Lucerne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucerne "Lucerne").[\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-20min.ch-26)

Switzerland is a [federal republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_republic "Federal republic") composed of [26 cantons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantons_of_Switzerland "Cantons of Switzerland"), with Bern serving as the [federal city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_city "Federal city") and the seat of the national government.[\[a\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-fedcit-2)[\[3\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-deFacto-4)[\[2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Bundesstadt-3) The country encompasses four principal linguistic and cultural regions— [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German-speaking_Switzerland "German-speaking Switzerland"), [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romandy "Romandy"), [Italian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_speaking_Swiss "Italian speaking Swiss"), and [Romansh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romansh_language "Romansh language")—reflecting a long-standing tradition of multilingualism and cultural pluralism. Swiss national identity nonetheless remains fairly cohesive, rooted in a shared historical background, common values such as [federalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalism "Federalism") and [direct democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_democracy "Direct democracy"),[\[20\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-27) and [Alpine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alps "Alps") symbolism.[\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Zimmer-2004-28)[\[22\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-29) Swiss [nationhood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation "Nation") transcends language, ethnicity, and religion, leading to Switzerland being described as a _[Willensnation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Willensnation "Willensnation")_ ("nation of volition") rather than a conventional [nation state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nation_state "Nation state").[\[23\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-30)

The country originates from the [Old Swiss Confederacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Swiss_Confederacy "Old Swiss Confederacy") established in the [Late Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Late_Middle_Ages "Late Middle Ages") as a [defensive and commercial alliance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eidgenossenschaft "Eidgenossenschaft"); the [Federal Charter of 1291](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Charter_of_1291 "Federal Charter of 1291") is considered the country's founding document. The confederation steadily [expanded and consolidated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Growth_of_the_Old_Swiss_Confederacy "Growth of the Old Swiss Confederacy") despite external threats and internal [political and religious strife](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformation_in_Switzerland "Reformation in Switzerland").[\[24\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-31) Swiss independence from the [Holy Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Roman_Empire "Holy Roman Empire") was formally recognized in the [Peace of Westphalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_of_Westphalia "Peace of Westphalia") in 1648. The confederation was among the first and few republics of the [early modern period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_modern_period "Early modern period"), and the only one besides [San Marino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/San_Marino "San Marino") to survive the [Napoleonic Wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleonic_Wars "Napoleonic Wars").[\[25\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-32) Switzerland remained a network of self-governing states until 1798, when [revolutionary France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolution "French Revolution") invaded and [imposed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_invasion_of_Switzerland "French invasion of Switzerland") the [centralist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centralist "Centralist") [Helvetic Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helvetic_Republic "Helvetic Republic"). [Napoleon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon "Napoleon") abolished the republic in 1803 and [reinstated a confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Act_of_Mediation "Act of Mediation").[\[26\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-33) Following the Napoleonic Wars, Switzerland [restored its pre-revolutionary system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restoration_and_Regeneration_in_Switzerland "Restoration and Regeneration in Switzerland"), but [by 1830](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutions_of_1830 "Revolutions of 1830") faced growing division [and conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonderbund_War "Sonderbund War") between liberal and conservative movements; this culminated in [a new constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Federal_Constitution "Swiss Federal Constitution") in 1848 that established the current federal system and enshrined principles such as individual rights, separation of powers, and parliamentary bicameralism.[\[27\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-34)

Switzerland has maintained a [policy of armed neutrality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_neutrality "Swiss neutrality") since the 16th century and has not fought an international war [since 1815](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland_in_the_Napoleonic_era "Switzerland in the Napoleonic era"). It joined the [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe "Council of Europe") in 1964 and the [United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations "United Nations") (UN) in 2002, pursuing an active foreign policy that includes frequent involvement in [peace building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_building "Peace building") and [global governance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_governance "Global governance"). Switzerland is the birthplace of the [Red Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Committee_of_the_Red_Cross "International Committee of the Red Cross") and hosts the headquarters or offices of most major [international institutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_organisations_in_Europe "International organisations in Europe"), including the [WTO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WTO "WTO"), the [WHO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WHO "WHO"), the [ILO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ILO "ILO"), [FIFA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIFA "FIFA"), the [WEF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WEF "WEF"), and the UN. It is a founding member of the [European Free Trade Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Free_Trade_Association "European Free Trade Association") (EFTA) and participates in the [European single market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_single_market "European single market") and the [Schengen Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schengen_Area "Schengen Area"). Switzerland is among the world's [most developed countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_Human_Development_Index "List of countries by Human Development Index"), with the highest nominal [wealth per adult](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wealth_per_adult "Wealth per adult")[\[28\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-35) and the [eighth-highest gross domestic product (GDP) per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_(PPP)_per_capita "List of countries by GDP (PPP) per capita").[\[29\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-36)[\[30\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-37) [It performs highly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_rankings_of_Switzerland "International rankings of Switzerland") on several international metrics, including [economic competitiveness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Competitiveness_Report "Global Competitiveness Report"), [democratic governance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_Index "Democracy Index"), and [press freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Press_freedom "Press freedom"). Zurich, Geneva and Basel rank among the highest in [quality of life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quality_of_life "Quality of life"),[\[31\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-38)[\[32\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-39) albeit with some of the highest [costs of living](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Costs_of_living "Costs of living").[\[33\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-40) Switzerland has a longstanding [banking and financial sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banking_in_Switzerland "Banking in Switzerland"), advanced [pharmaceutical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharmaceutical_industry_in_Switzerland "Pharmaceutical industry in Switzerland") and biotechnology industries, and a strong tradition of [watchmaking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_watchmaking_industry "Swiss watchmaking industry"), precision engineering, and technology. It is known for its [chocolate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_chocolate "Swiss chocolate") and [cheese production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss-type_cheeses "Swiss-type cheeses"), well-developed [tourism industry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Switzerland "Tourism in Switzerland"), and growing startup sector.

## Etymology

Main article: [Name of Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Name_of_Switzerland "Name of Switzerland")

The English name _Switzerland_ compounds _Switzer_, an obsolete term for a [Swiss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_people "Swiss people") person which was in use during the 16th to 19th centuries,[\[34\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-41) with the common English suffix [-land](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/-land "wikt:-land") denoting a region or country. The English adjective _Swiss_ is a [loanword](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loanword "Loanword") from French __Suisse__, also in use since the 16th century. The name _Switzer_ is from the [Alemannic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alemannic_German "Alemannic German") __Schwiizer__, in origin an inhabitant of _[Schwyz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schwyz "Schwyz")_ and its [associated territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canton_of_Schwyz "Canton of Schwyz"), one of the _[Waldstätte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waldst%C3%A4tte "Waldstätte")_ cantons which formed the nucleus of the [Old Swiss Confederacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Swiss_Confederacy "Old Swiss Confederacy"). The Swiss began to adopt the name for themselves after the [Swabian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swabian_War "Swabian War") of 1499, used alongside the term for "Confederates", _[Eidgenossen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eidgenossen "Eidgenossen")_ (literally: _comrades by oath_), used since the 14th century. The [ISO 3166-1 data codes for Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Data_codes_for_Switzerland#Country "Data codes for Switzerland"), CH and CHE, are derived from [Latin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Latin "Latin") _[Confoederatio Helvetica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confoederatio_Helvetica "Confoederatio Helvetica")_ ( _Helvetic Confederation_).

The toponym _Schwyz_ itself was first attested in 972, as [Old High German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_High_German "Old High German") __Suittes__, perhaps related to __swedan__ 'to burn' (cf. [Old Norse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Norse "Old Norse") _svíða_ 'to singe, burn'), referring to the area of forest that was burned and cleared to build.[\[35\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-42) The name was extended to the area dominated by the canton, and after the Swabian War of 1499 gradually came to be used for the entire Confederation.[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-43)[\[37\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-44) The [Swiss German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_German "Swiss German") name of the country, __Schwiiz__, is homophonous to that of the canton and the settlement, but distinguished by the use of the definite article ( __d'Schwiiz__ for the Confederation,[\[38\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-45) but simply __Schwyz__ for the canton and the town).[\[39\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-46) The long \[iː\] of Swiss German is historically and still often today spelled ⟨y⟩ rather than ⟨ii⟩, preserving the original identity of the two names even in writing.

The Latin name _Confoederatio Helvetica_ was [neologised](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neologism "Neologism") and introduced gradually after the [formation of the federal state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland_as_a_federal_state "Switzerland as a federal state") in 1848, harking back to the Napoleonic [Helvetic Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helvetic_Republic "Helvetic Republic"). It appeared on coins from 1879, inscribed on the [Federal Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Palace "Federal Palace") in 1902 and after 1948 used in the official seal[\[40\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-47) (e.g., the [ISO banking code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISO_4217 "ISO 4217") "CHF" for the [Swiss franc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_franc "Swiss franc"), the Swiss postage stamps ('HELVETIA') and the country top-level domain ".ch", are both taken from the state's Latin name). The name _Helvetica_ is derived from the _[Helvetii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helvetii "Helvetii")_, a [Gaulish tribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaulish_tribe "Gaulish tribe") living on the [Swiss Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Plateau "Swiss Plateau") before the [Roman era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland_in_the_Roman_era "Switzerland in the Roman era"). It in turn, gives its name to a [typeface](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helvetica "Helvetica") developed in 1960.[\[41\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-48)

_[Helvetia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helvetia "Helvetia")_ appeared as a [national personification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_personification "National personification") of the Swiss confederacy in the 17th century in a 1672 play by Johann Caspar Weissenbach.[\[42\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-49)

## History

Main article: [History of Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Switzerland "History of Switzerland")

The state of Switzerland took its present form with the adoption of the [Swiss Federal Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Federal_Constitution "Swiss Federal Constitution") in 1848. Switzerland's precursors established a defensive alliance in 1291, forming a loose [confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confederation "Confederation") that persisted for centuries.

### Beginnings

Main articles: [Early history of Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_history_of_Switzerland "Early history of Switzerland") and [Switzerland in the Roman era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland_in_the_Roman_era "Switzerland in the Roman era")

The oldest traces of hominid existence in Switzerland date to about 150,000 years ago.[\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Early-50) The oldest known farming settlements in Switzerland, which were found at [Gächlingen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/G%C3%A4chlingen "Gächlingen"), date to around 5300 BC.[\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Early-50)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a8/Theater_Kaiseraugst.jpg/500px-Theater_Kaiseraugst.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Theater_Kaiseraugst.jpg) Founded in 44 BC by [Lucius Munatius Plancus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucius_Munatius_Plancus "Lucius Munatius Plancus"), [Augusta Raurica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augusta_Raurica "Augusta Raurica") (near Basel) was the first Roman settlement on the Rhine and is now among the most important archaeological sites in Switzerland.[\[44\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-51)

The earliest known tribes formed the [Hallstatt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hallstatt_culture "Hallstatt culture") and [La Tène cultures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_T%C3%A8ne_culture "La Tène culture"), named after the archaeological site of La Tène on the north side of [Lake Neuchâtel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Neuch%C3%A2tel "Lake Neuchâtel"). La Tène culture developed and flourished during the late [Iron Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron_Age "Iron Age") from around 450 BC,[\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Early-50) possibly influenced by [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Greek "Ancient Greek") and [Etruscan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Etruscan_civilization "Etruscan civilization") civilisations. One of the most prominent La Tène tribes were the [Helvetii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helvetii "Helvetii"), who primarily occupied the [Swiss Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Plateau "Swiss Plateau"), alongside the [Rhaetians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhaetians "Rhaetians") in the eastern regions. Facing pressures from Germanic tribes, in 58 BC, the Helvetii, influenced by [Orgetorix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orgetorix "Orgetorix"), a wealthy aristocrat, decided to abandon the Swiss Plateau for better opportunities in western Gallia. After Orgetorix's mysterious death, the tribe continued their migration but was decisively defeated by Julius Caesar's armies at the [Battle of Bibracte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Bibracte "Battle of Bibracte"), in present-day eastern France. Following their defeat, the Helvetii were forced by Caesar to return to their original lands, where they were subjected to stringent restrictions on their autonomy and movements.[\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Early-50) In 15 BC, [Tiberius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tiberius "Tiberius") (later the second Roman emperor) and his brother [Drusus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nero_Claudius_Drusus "Nero Claudius Drusus") conquered the Alps, integrating them into the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire "Roman Empire"). The area occupied by the Helvetii first became part of Rome's [Gallia Belgica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallia_Belgica "Gallia Belgica") province and then of its [Germania Superior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germania_Superior "Germania Superior") province. The eastern portion of modern Switzerland was integrated into the [Roman province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_province "Roman province") of [Raetia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raetia "Raetia"). Sometime around the start of the [Common Era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_Era "Common Era"), the Romans maintained a large camp called [Vindonissa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vindonissa "Vindonissa"), now a ruin at the confluence of the [Aare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aare "Aare") and [Reuss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reuss_(river) "Reuss (river)") rivers, near the town of [Windisch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Windisch,_Switzerland "Windisch, Switzerland").[\[45\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-52)

The first and second century AD was an age of prosperity on the Swiss Plateau. Towns such as [Aventicum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aventicum "Aventicum"), [Iulia Equestris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iulia_Equestris "Iulia Equestris") and [Augusta Raurica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augusta_Raurica "Augusta Raurica") reached a remarkable size, while hundreds of agricultural estates ( [Villae rusticae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Villae_rusticae "Villae rusticae")) were established in the countryside.[\[46\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-:1-53)

Around 260 AD, the fall of the [Agri Decumates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agri_Decumates "Agri Decumates") territory north of the [Rhine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhine "Rhine") transformed today's Switzerland into a frontier land of the Empire. Repeated raids by the [Alamanni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alamanni "Alamanni") tribes provoked the ruin of the Roman towns and economy, forcing the population to shelter near Roman fortresses, like the [Castrum Rauracense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Castrum_Rauracense "Castrum Rauracense") near Augusta Raurica. The Empire built another line of defence at the north border (the so-called Donau-Iller-Rhine-Limes). At the end of the fourth century, the increased Germanic pressure forced the Romans to abandon the linear defence concept. The Swiss Plateau was finally open to [Germanic tribes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germanic_tribes "Germanic tribes").\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

In the [Early Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Middle_Ages "Early Middle Ages"), from the end of the fourth century, the western extent of modern-day Switzerland was part of the territory of the [Kings of the Burgundians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Burgundy "Kingdom of Burgundy"), who introduced the French language to the area. The [Alemanni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alemanni "Alemanni") settled the Swiss Plateau in the fifth century and the [valleys of the Alps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valleys_of_the_Alps "Valleys of the Alps") in the eighth century, forming [Alemannia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alemannia "Alemannia"). Modern-day Switzerland was then divided between the kingdoms of Alemannia and [Burgundy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burgundy "Burgundy").[\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Early-50) The entire region became part of the expanding [Frankish Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frankish_Empire "Frankish Empire") in the sixth century, following [Clovis I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clovis_I "Clovis I")'s victory over the Alemanni at [Tolbiac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tolbiac "Tolbiac") in 504 AD, and later Frankish domination of the Burgundians.[\[47\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Nationsencyclopedia-Hist-54)[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Nationsonline-55)

In the sixth to eighth centuries, Swiss regions continued under Frankish hegemony ( [Merovingian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merovingian "Merovingian") and [Carolingian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carolingian_Empire "Carolingian Empire") dynasties) but after its extension under [Charlemagne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charlemagne "Charlemagne"), the [Frankish Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frankish_Empire "Frankish Empire") was divided by the [Treaty of Verdun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treaty_of_Verdun "Treaty of Verdun") in 843.[\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Early-50) The territories of present-day Switzerland became divided into [Middle Francia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Francia "Middle Francia") and [East Francia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_Francia "East Francia") until they were reunified under the [Holy Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Roman_Empire "Holy Roman Empire") around 1000AD.[\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Early-50)

In the 10th century, as the rule of the Carolingians waned, [Magyars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magyars "Magyars") destroyed [Basel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basel "Basel") in 917 and [St. Gallen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Gallen "St. Gallen") in 926. In response, [Henry the Fowler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_the_Fowler "Henry the Fowler"), the then ruler of East Francia, decreed the fortification of key settlements to defend against these invasions. Large villages and towns, including strategic locations like Zurich and St.Gallen, were fortified. This initiative led to the development of what were essentially early urban strongholds and city governments in Eastern Switzerland.[\[46\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-:1-53)

By 1200, the Swiss Plateau comprised the dominions of the houses of [Savoy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Savoy "House of Savoy"), [Zähringer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Z%C3%A4hringer "Zähringer"), [Habsburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habsburg "Habsburg"), and [Kyburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Kyburg "House of Kyburg").[\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Early-50) Some regions ( [Uri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canton_of_Uri "Canton of Uri"), [Schwyz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canton_of_Schwyz "Canton of Schwyz"), [Unterwalden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unterwalden "Unterwalden"), later known as _[Waldstätten](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waldst%C3%A4tte "Waldstätte")_) were accorded the [Imperial immediacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imperial_immediacy "Imperial immediacy") to grant the empire direct control over the mountain passes. With the extinction of its male line in 1263, the Kyburg dynasty fell in AD 1264. The Habsburgs under [King Rudolph I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudolph_I_of_Germany "Rudolph I of Germany") (Holy Roman Emperor in 1273) laid claim to the Kyburg lands and annexed them, extending their territory to the eastern Swiss Plateau.[\[47\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Nationsencyclopedia-Hist-54)

### Old Swiss Confederacy

Main article: [Old Swiss Confederacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Swiss_Confederacy "Old Swiss Confederacy")

Further information: [Growth of the Old Swiss Confederacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Growth_of_the_Old_Swiss_Confederacy "Growth of the Old Swiss Confederacy"), [Reformation in Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformation_in_Switzerland "Reformation in Switzerland"), and [Early Modern Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Modern_Switzerland "Early Modern Switzerland")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Old_Swiss_Confederation.jpg/500px-Old_Swiss_Confederation.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Old_Swiss_Confederation.jpg) The Old Swiss Confederacy from 1291 (dark green) to the sixteenth century (light green) and its associates (blue). In the other colours shown are the subject territories.[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Bundesbrief.jpg/500px-Bundesbrief.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bundesbrief.jpg) The 1291 _Bundesbrief_ (federal charter)

The Old Swiss Confederacy was an alliance among the valley communities of the central Alps. The Confederacy was governed by [nobles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_nobility "Swiss nobility") and [patricians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patrician_(post-Roman_Europe) "Patrician (post-Roman Europe)") of various cantons who facilitated management of common interests and ensured peace on mountain trade routes. The [Federal Charter of 1291](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Charter_of_1291 "Federal Charter of 1291") is considered the confederacy's founding document, even though similar alliances likely existed decades earlier. The document was agreed among the [rural communes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_commune "Medieval commune") of [Uri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canton_of_Uri "Canton of Uri"), [Schwyz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canton_of_Schwyz "Canton of Schwyz"), and [Unterwalden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unterwalden "Unterwalden").[\[49\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-56)\[ _[pageneeded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources "Wikipedia:Citing sources")_\][\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Brief-57)

By 1353, the three original [cantons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantons_of_Switzerland "Cantons of Switzerland") had joined with the cantons of [Glarus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canton_of_Glarus "Canton of Glarus") and [Zug](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canton_of_Zug "Canton of Zug") and the [Lucerne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucerne "Lucerne"), [Zurich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zurich "Zurich") and [Bern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bern "Bern") city-states to form the "Old Confederacy" of eight states that obtained through the end of the 15th century.[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Brief-57) The expansion led to increased power and wealth for the confederation. By 1460, the confederates controlled most of the territory south and west of the Rhine to the Alps and the [Jura Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jura_Mountains "Jura Mountains"), and the [University of Basel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Basel "University of Basel") was founded (with a faculty of medicine) establishing a tradition of chemical and medical research. This increased after victories against the Habsburgs ( [Battle of Sempach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Sempach "Battle of Sempach"), [Battle of Näfels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_N%C3%A4fels "Battle of Näfels")), over [Charles the Bold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_the_Bold "Charles the Bold") of [Burgundy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duchy_of_Burgundy "Duchy of Burgundy") during the 1470s, and the success of the [Swiss mercenaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_mercenaries "Swiss mercenaries"). The Swiss victory in the [Swabian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swabian_War "Swabian War") against the [Swabian League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swabian_League "Swabian League") of [Emperor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Roman_Emperor "Holy Roman Emperor") [Maximilian I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maximilian_I,_Holy_Roman_Emperor "Maximilian I, Holy Roman Emperor") in 1499 amounted to _de facto_ independence within the [Holy Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_Roman_Empire "Holy Roman Empire").[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Brief-57) In 1501, Basel[\[51\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-58) and Schaffhausen joined the Old Swiss Confederacy.[\[52\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-59)

The Confederacy acquired a reputation of invincibility during these earlier wars, but [expansion of the confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Growth_of_the_Old_Swiss_Confederacy "Growth of the Old Swiss Confederacy") suffered a setback in 1515 with the Swiss defeat in the [Battle of Marignano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Marignano "Battle of Marignano"). This ended the so-called "heroic" epoch of Swiss history.[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Brief-57) The success of [Zwingli](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zwingli "Zwingli")'s [Reformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformation_in_Switzerland "Reformation in Switzerland") in some cantons led to inter-cantonal religious conflicts in 1529 and 1531 ( [Wars of Kappel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wars_of_Kappel "Wars of Kappel")). It was not until more than one hundred years after these internal wars that, in 1648, under the [Peace of Westphalia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_of_Westphalia "Peace of Westphalia"), European countries recognised Switzerland's independence from the Holy Roman Empire and its [neutrality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutral_country "Neutral country").[\[47\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Nationsencyclopedia-Hist-54)[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Nationsonline-55)

During the [Early Modern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Modern_Switzerland "Early Modern Switzerland") period of Swiss history, the growing authoritarianism of the patriciate families[\[53\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-60) combined with a financial crisis in the wake of the [Thirty Years' War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thirty_Years'_War "Thirty Years' War") led to the [Swiss peasant war of 1653](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_peasant_war_of_1653 "Swiss peasant war of 1653"). In the background to this struggle, the conflict between [Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholicism_in_Switzerland "Roman Catholicism in Switzerland") and [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism_in_Switzerland "Protestantism in Switzerland") cantons persisted, erupting in further violence at the [First War of Villmergen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_War_of_Villmergen "First War of Villmergen"), in 1656, and the [Toggenburg War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toggenburg_War "Toggenburg War") (or Second War of Villmergen), in 1712.[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Brief-57)

### Napoleonic era

Main articles: [Switzerland in the Napoleonic era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland_in_the_Napoleonic_era "Switzerland in the Napoleonic era"), [Helvetic Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helvetic_Republic "Helvetic Republic"), and [Act of Mediation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Act_of_Mediation "Act of Mediation")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9d/Acte_de_M%C3%A9diation_mg_0643.jpg/500px-Acte_de_M%C3%A9diation_mg_0643.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Acte_de_M%C3%A9diation_mg_0643.jpg) The Act of Mediation was Napoleon's attempt at a compromise between the [Ancien Régime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancien_R%C3%A9gime "Ancien Régime") and a Republic.

In 1798, the [revolutionary French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Revolution "French Revolution") government invaded Switzerland and imposed a new unified constitution.[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Brief-57) This centralised the government of the country, effectively abolishing the cantons: moreover, [Mülhausen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%BClhausen "Mülhausen") left Switzerland and the [Valtellina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valtellina "Valtellina") valley became part of the [Cisalpine Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cisalpine_Republic "Cisalpine Republic"). The new regime, known as the Helvetic Republic, was highly unpopular. An invading foreign army had imposed and destroyed centuries of tradition, making Switzerland nothing more than a French [satellite state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satellite_state "Satellite state"). The fierce French suppression of the [Nidwalden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nidwalden "Nidwalden") Revolt in September 1798 was an example of the oppressive presence of the [French Army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Army "French Army") and the local population's resistance to the occupation.[\[54\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-61)

When war broke out between France and its rivals, Russian and [Austrian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Habsburg_monarchy "Habsburg monarchy") forces invaded Switzerland. The Swiss refused to fight alongside the French in the name of the Helvetic Republic. In 1803 [Napoleon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Napoleon "Napoleon") organised a meeting of the leading Swiss politicians from both sides in Paris. The [Act of Mediation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Act_of_Mediation "Act of Mediation") was the result, which largely restored Swiss autonomy and introduced a Confederation of 19 cantons.[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Brief-57) Henceforth, much of Swiss politics would concern balancing the cantons' tradition of self-rule with the need for a central government.[\[55\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-62)

In 1815, the [Congress of Vienna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Vienna "Congress of Vienna") fully re-established Swiss independence, and the European powers recognised permanent Swiss neutrality.[\[47\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Nationsencyclopedia-Hist-54)[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Nationsonline-55)[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Brief-57) Swiss troops served foreign governments until 1860 when they fought in the [siege of Gaeta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Gaeta_(1860) "Siege of Gaeta (1860)"). The treaty allowed Switzerland to increase its territory, with the admission of the cantons of [Valais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valais "Valais"), [Neuchâtel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canton_of_Neuch%C3%A2tel "Canton of Neuchâtel") and [Geneva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canton_of_Geneva "Canton of Geneva"). Switzerland's borders saw only minor adjustments thereafter.[\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-63)

### Federal state

Main articles: [Restoration and Regeneration (Switzerland)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Restoration_and_Regeneration_(Switzerland) "Restoration and Regeneration (Switzerland)") and [Switzerland as a federal state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland_as_a_federal_state "Switzerland as a federal state")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f4/Bern%2C_Federal_Palace%2C_1857.jpg/500px-Bern%2C_Federal_Palace%2C_1857.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bern,_Federal_Palace,_1857.jpg) The first [Federal Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Palace "Federal Palace") in Bern (1857). One of the three cantons presiding over the [Tagsatzung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagsatzung "Tagsatzung") (former legislative and executive council), Bern was chosen as the permanent seat of federal legislative and executive institutions in 1848, in part because of its closeness to the French-speaking area.[\[2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Bundesstadt-3)

The restoration of power to the patriciate was only temporary. After a period of unrest with repeated violent clashes, such as the [Züriputsch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Z%C3%BCriputsch "Züriputsch") of 1839, civil war (the _[Sonderbundskrieg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonderbundskrieg "Sonderbundskrieg")_) broke out in 1847, when some Catholic cantons tried to set up a separate alliance (the _Sonderbund_).[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Brief-57) The war lasted less than a month, causing fewer than 100 casualties, most of which were through [friendly fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friendly_fire "Friendly fire"). The _Sonderbundskrieg_ had a significant impact on the psychology and society of Switzerland.[\[57\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-64)

The war convinced most Swiss of the need for unity and strength. Swiss from all strata of society, whether Catholic or Protestant, from the liberal or conservative current, realised that the cantons would profit more from merging their economic and religious interests.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

Thus, while the rest of Europe saw [revolutionary uprisings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revolutions_of_1848 "Revolutions of 1848"), the Swiss created a constitution that provided for a [federal layout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Federal_Constitution "Swiss Federal Constitution"), much of it inspired by the [United States Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_the_United_States "Constitution of the United States"). This new constitution provided central authority while leaving the cantons the right to self-government on local issues. Giving credit to those who favoured the power of the cantons (the Sonderbund Kantone), the national assembly was divided between an [upper house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Upper_house "Upper house") (the [Council of States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Council_of_States "Swiss Council of States"), two representatives per canton) and a [lower house](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_house "Lower house") (the [National Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Council_of_Switzerland "National Council of Switzerland"), with representatives elected from across the country). [Referendums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Referendum "Referendum") were made mandatory for any amendments.[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Nationsonline-55) This new constitution ended the legal power of [nobility in Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_nobility#Current_situation "Swiss nobility").[\[58\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-65)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a9/Gotthard_Er%C3%B6ffnungszug_Bellinzona.jpg/500px-Gotthard_Er%C3%B6ffnungszug_Bellinzona.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Gotthard_Er%C3%B6ffnungszug_Bellinzona.jpg) Inauguration in 1882 of the [Gotthard rail tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gotthard_rail_tunnel "Gotthard rail tunnel") connecting the southern canton of Ticino, the longest in the world at the time

A single system of weights and measures was introduced, and in 1850, the [Swiss franc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_franc "Swiss franc") became the Swiss [single currency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single_currency "Single currency"), complemented by the WIR franc in 1934.[\[59\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-66) Article 11 of the constitution forbade sending troops to serve abroad, marking the end of foreign service. It came with the exception of serving the [Holy See](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_See "Holy See"), and the Swiss were still obliged to serve [Francis II of the Two Sicilies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Francis_II_of_the_Two_Sicilies "Francis II of the Two Sicilies"), with Swiss Guards present at the [siege of Gaeta in 1860](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Siege_of_Gaeta_(1860) "Siege of Gaeta (1860)").\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

An important clause of the constitution was that it could be entirely rewritten, if necessary, thus enabling it to evolve as a whole rather than being modified one amendment at a time.[\[60\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-67)\[ _[pageneeded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources "Wikipedia:Citing sources")_\]

This need soon proved itself when the rise in population and the [Industrial Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_Revolution "Industrial Revolution") that followed led to calls to modify the constitution accordingly. The population rejected an early draft in 1872, but modifications led to its acceptance in 1874.[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Brief-57) It introduced the [facultative referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Referendum#Switzerland "Referendum") for laws at the federal level. It also established federal responsibility for defence, trade, and legal matters.

In 1891, the constitution was revised with uncommonly strong elements of [direct democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_democracy "Direct democracy"), which remain unique today.[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Brief-57)

### Modern history

Main articles: [Switzerland during the world wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland_during_the_world_wars "Switzerland during the world wars") and [Modern history of Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_history_of_Switzerland "Modern history of Switzerland")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c3/Ulrich_Wille.jpg/500px-Ulrich_Wille.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Ulrich_Wille.jpg) General [Ulrich Wille](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulrich_Wille "Ulrich Wille"), appointed commander-in-chief of the Swiss Army for the duration of World War I

Switzerland was not invaded during either of the world wars. During [World War I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_I "World War I"), Switzerland was home to the revolutionary and founder of the [Soviet Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soviet_Union "Soviet Union") Vladimir Illych Ulyanov ( [Vladimir Lenin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Lenin "Vladimir Lenin")) who remained there until 1917.[\[61\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-68) Swiss neutrality was seriously questioned by the short-lived [Grimm–Hoffmann affair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grimm%E2%80%93Hoffmann_affair "Grimm–Hoffmann affair") in 1917. In 1920, Switzerland joined the [League of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Nations "League of Nations"), which was based in [Geneva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva "Geneva"), after it was exempted from military requirements.[\[62\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-:0-69)

During [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II "World War II"), [detailed invasion plans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Tannenbaum "Operation Tannenbaum") were drawn up by the Germans,[\[63\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-70) but Switzerland was never attacked.[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Brief-57) Switzerland was able to remain independent through a combination of military deterrence, concessions to Germany, and good fortune, as larger events during the war intervened.[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Nationsonline-55)[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Stonebrooks-71) General [Henri Guisan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henri_Guisan "Henri Guisan"), appointed the [commander-in-chief for the duration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_(Switzerland) "General (Switzerland)") of the war, ordered a general mobilisation of the armed forces. The Swiss military strategy changed from static defence at the borders to organised long-term attrition and withdrawal to strong, well-stockpiled positions high in the Alps, known as the [Reduit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Redoubt_(Switzerland) "National Redoubt (Switzerland)"). Switzerland was an important base for espionage by both sides and often mediated communications between the [Axis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_Powers "Axis Powers") and [Allied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II "Allies of World War II") powers.[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Stonebrooks-71)

Switzerland's trade was blockaded by both the Allies and the Axis. Economic cooperation with [Nazi Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Germany "Nazi Germany"); such as purchasing gold (enabling Germany to purchase foreign goods) and extension of credit varied according to the perceived likelihood of invasion and the availability of other trading partners.[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-UEK_Report-72) Concessions reached a peak after a crucial rail link through [Vichy France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vichy_France "Vichy France") was severed in 1942, leaving Switzerland (together with [Liechtenstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liechtenstein "Liechtenstein")) entirely isolated from the wider world by Axis-controlled territory. Over the course of the war, Switzerland interned over 300,000 refugees[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Asylum-73) aided by the [International Red Cross](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Red_Cross "International Red Cross"), based in Geneva. Strict immigration and [asylum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_of_asylum "Right of asylum") policies and the financial relationships with Nazi Germany became controversial by the end of the 20th century.[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-UEK_Report-72):521

During the war, the Swiss Air Force engaged aircraft of both sides, shooting down 11 intruding [Luftwaffe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luftwaffe "Luftwaffe") planes in May and June 1940, then forcing down other intruders after a change of policy following threats from Germany. Over 100 Allied bombers and their crews were interned. Between 1940 and 1945, [Switzerland was bombed by the Allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bombings_of_Switzerland_in_World_War_II "Bombings of Switzerland in World War II"), causing fatalities and property damage.[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Stonebrooks-71) Among the cities and towns bombed were [Basel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basel "Basel"), [Brusio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brusio "Brusio"), [Chiasso](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiasso "Chiasso"), [Cornol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornol "Cornol"), Geneva, [Koblenz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koblenz,_Switzerland "Koblenz, Switzerland"), [Niederweningen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Niederweningen "Niederweningen"), [Rafz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rafz "Rafz"), [Renens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renens "Renens"), [Samedan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samedan "Samedan"), [Schaffhausen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schaffhausen "Schaffhausen"), [Stein am Rhein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stein_am_Rhein "Stein am Rhein"), [Tägerwilen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T%C3%A4gerwilen "Tägerwilen"), [Thayngen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thayngen "Thayngen"), [Vals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vals,_Switzerland "Vals, Switzerland"), and Zurich. Allied forces maintained that the bombings, which violated the 96th [Article of War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_of_War "Article of War"), resulted from navigation errors, equipment failure, weather conditions, and pilot errors. The Swiss expressed fear and concern that the bombings were intended to put pressure on Switzerland to end economic cooperation and neutrality with Nazi Germany.[\[67\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-74) Court-martial proceedings took place in England. The US paid SFR 62M for reparations.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

Switzerland's attitude towards [refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland_during_the_World_Wars#Refugees "Switzerland during the World Wars") was complicated and controversial; over the course of the war, it admitted as many as 300,000 refugees[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Asylum-73) while refusing tens of thousands more,[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-UEK_Report-72):107 including Jews persecuted by the Nazis.[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-UEK_Report-72):114

After the war, the Swiss government exported credits through the charitable fund known as the _Schweizerspende_ and donated to the [Marshall Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marshall_Plan "Marshall Plan") to help Europe's recovery, efforts that ultimately benefited the [Swiss economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_economy "Swiss economy").[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-UEK_Report-72):521

During the [Cold War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cold_War "Cold War"), Swiss authorities [considered the construction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland_and_weapons_of_mass_destruction "Switzerland and weapons of mass destruction") of a Swiss [nuclear bomb](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_bomb "Nuclear bomb").[\[68\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-75) Leading nuclear physicists at the [Federal Institute of Technology Zurich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Institute_of_Technology_Zurich "Federal Institute of Technology Zurich") such as [Paul Scherrer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Scherrer "Paul Scherrer") made this a realistic possibility.[\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-76) In 1988, the [Paul Scherrer Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Scherrer_Institute "Paul Scherrer Institute") was founded in his name to explore the therapeutic uses of [neutron scattering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neutron_scattering "Neutron scattering") technologies.[\[70\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-77) Financial problems with the defence budget and ethical considerations prevented the substantial funds from being allocated, and the [Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_Non-Proliferation_Treaty "Nuclear Non-Proliferation Treaty") of 1968 was seen as a valid alternative. Plans for building nuclear weapons were dropped by 1988.[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-78) Switzerland joined the [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe "Council of Europe") in 1963.[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Nationsonline-55)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/Bundesrat_der_Schweiz_2003.jpg/500px-Bundesrat_der_Schweiz_2003.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bundesrat_der_Schweiz_2003.jpg) In 2003, by granting the [Swiss People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_People's_Party "Swiss People's Party") a second seat in the governing cabinet, the Parliament altered the [coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magic_formula_(Swiss_politics) "Magic formula (Swiss politics)") that had dominated Swiss politics since 1959.

Switzerland was the last Western republic (the [Principality of Liechtenstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Principality_of_Liechtenstein "Principality of Liechtenstein") followed in 1984) to [grant women the right to vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Women's_suffrage_in_Switzerland "Women's suffrage in Switzerland"). Some Swiss cantons approved this in 1959, while at the federal level, it was achieved in 1971 and, after resistance, in the last canton [Appenzell Innerrhoden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appenzell_Innerrhoden "Appenzell Innerrhoden") (one of only two remaining _[Landsgemeinde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landsgemeinde "Landsgemeinde")_, along with [Glarus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canton_of_Glarus "Canton of Glarus")) in 1990.[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Brief-57)[\[72\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-79) After obtaining suffrage at the federal level, women quickly rose in political significance. The first woman on the seven-member [Federal Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Council_(Switzerland) "Federal Council (Switzerland)") executive was [Elisabeth Kopp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elisabeth_Kopp "Elisabeth Kopp"), who served from 1984 to 1989,[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Brief-57) and the first female president was [Ruth Dreifuss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruth_Dreifuss "Ruth Dreifuss") in 1999.[\[73\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-80)

In 1979 areas from the canton of [Bern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bern "Bern") attained independence from the Bernese, forming the new [canton of Jura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canton_of_Jura "Canton of Jura"). On 18 April 1999, the Swiss population and the cantons voted in favour of a completely revised [federal constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Federal_Constitution "Swiss Federal Constitution").[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Brief-57)

In 2002 Switzerland became a full member of the United Nations, leaving [Vatican City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holy_See "Holy See") as the last widely recognised state without full UN membership.[\[74\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-81) Switzerland is a founding member of the [EFTA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EFTA "EFTA") but not the [European Economic Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Economic_Area "European Economic Area") (EEA). An application for membership in the [European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union "European Union") was sent in May 1992, but did not advance since rejecting the EEA in December 1992[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Brief-57) when Switzerland conducted a referendum on the EEA. Several referendums on the EU issue ensued; due to opposition from the citizens, the membership application was withdrawn. Nonetheless, Swiss law is gradually changing to conform with that of the EU, and the government signed [bilateral agreements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bilateralism "Bilateralism") with the European Union. Switzerland, together with Liechtenstein, has been surrounded by the EU since Austria's entry in 1995. On 5 June 2005, Swiss voters agreed by a 55% majority to join the [Schengen treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schengen_treaty "Schengen treaty"), a result that EU commentators regarded as a sign of support.[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Nationsonline-55) In September 2020, a referendum calling for a vote to end the pact that allowed a free movement of people from the EU was introduced by the [Swiss People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_People's_Party "Swiss People's Party") (SVP).[\[75\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-82) However, voters rejected the attempt to retake control of immigration, defeating the motion by a roughly 63%–37% margin.[\[76\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-83)

On 9 February 2014, 50.3% of Swiss voters approved a ballot [initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_initiative_(Switzerland) "Popular initiative (Switzerland)") launched by the Swiss People's Party (SVP/UDC) to [restrict immigration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_popular_initiative_%22Against_mass_immigration%22 "Federal popular initiative \"Against mass immigration\""). This initiative was mostly backed by rural (57.6% approval) and suburban groups (51.2% approval), and isolated towns (51.3% approval) as well as by a strong majority (69.2% approval) in Ticino, while metropolitan centres (58.5% rejection) and the French-speaking part (58.5% rejection) rejected it.[\[77\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-84) In December 2016, a political compromise with the EU was attained that eliminated quotas on EU citizens, but still allowed favourable treatment of Swiss-based job applicants.[\[78\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-85) On 27 September 2020, 62% of Swiss voters rejected the anti-free movement referendum by SVP.[\[79\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-86)

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Switzerland "Geography of Switzerland")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/ff/Reliefkarte_Schweiz.png/500px-Reliefkarte_Schweiz.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Reliefkarte_Schweiz.png) Physical map of Switzerland (in [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_language "German language"))

Extending across the north and south side of the [Alps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alps "Alps") in [west](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Europe "Western Europe")-central Europe, Switzerland encompasses diverse landscapes and climates across its 41,291 square kilometres (15,943sqmi).[\[9\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Territory_and_environment-12)

Switzerland lies between latitudes [45°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/45th_parallel_north "45th parallel north") and [48° N](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/48th_parallel_north "48th parallel north"), and longitudes [5°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/5th_meridian_east "5th meridian east") and [11° E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/11th_meridian_east "11th meridian east"). It contains three basic topographical areas: the [Swiss Alps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Alps "Swiss Alps") to the south, the Swiss Plateau or Central Plateau, and the [Jura Mountains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jura_Mountains "Jura Mountains") on the west. The Alps are a mountain range running across the central and south of the country, constituting about 60% of the country's area. The majority of the population live on the Swiss Plateau. The Swiss Alps host many glaciers, covering 1,063 square kilometres (410mi2). From these originate the headwaters of several major rivers, such as the [Rhine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhine "Rhine"), [Inn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inn_(river) "Inn (river)"), [Ticino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ticino_(river) "Ticino (river)") and [Rhône](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rh%C3%B4ne "Rhône"), which flow in the four cardinal directions, spreading across Europe. The hydrographic network includes several of the largest bodies of fresh water in Central and Western Europe, among which are [Lake Geneva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Geneva "Lake Geneva") (Lac Léman in French), [Lake Constance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Constance "Lake Constance") (Bodensee in German) and [Lake Maggiore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Maggiore "Lake Maggiore"). Switzerland has more than 1500 lakes and contains 6% of Europe's freshwater stock. Lakes and glaciers cover about 6% of the national territory. Lake Geneva is the largest lake and is shared with France. The Rhône is both the main source and outflow of Lake Geneva. Lake Constance is the second largest and, like Lake Geneva, an intermediate step by the Rhine at the border with Austria and Germany. While the Rhône flows into the [Mediterranean Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_Sea "Mediterranean Sea") at the French [Camargue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camargue "Camargue") region and the Rhine flows into the [North Sea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Sea "North Sea") at [Rotterdam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rotterdam "Rotterdam"), about 1,000 kilometres (620 miles) apart, both springs are only about 22 kilometres (14 miles) apart in the Swiss Alps.[\[80\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Geo-87)[\[81\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-88) 90% of Switzerland's 65,000-kilometre-long network of rivers and streams have been straightened, dammed, canalized or channelled underground, in an effort to prevent natural disasters such as flooding, landslides, and avalanches.[\[82\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-89) 80% of all Swiss drinking water comes from [groundwater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Groundwater "Groundwater") sources.[\[83\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-90)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9c/Haute_route%2C_panorama_version.jpg/960px-Haute_route%2C_panorama_version.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Haute_route,_panorama_version.jpg)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/View_from_Pilatus%2C_retouched.jpg/960px-View_from_Pilatus%2C_retouched.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:View_from_Pilatus,_retouched.jpg)

Contrasted landscapes between the regions of the [Matterhorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matterhorn "Matterhorn") and [Lake Lucerne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Lucerne "Lake Lucerne")

Forty-eight mountains are 4,000 metres (13,000ft) or higher in height.[\[80\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Geo-87) At 4,634m (15,203ft), [Monte Rosa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monte_Rosa "Monte Rosa") is the highest, although the [Matterhorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matterhorn "Matterhorn") (4,478m or 14,692ft) is the best known. Both are located within the [Pennine Alps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pennine_Alps "Pennine Alps") in the canton of [Valais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valais "Valais"), on the border with Italy. The section of the [Bernese Alps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernese_Alps "Bernese Alps") above the deep glacial [Lauterbrunnen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lauterbrunnen "Lauterbrunnen") valley, containing 72 waterfalls, is well known for the [Jungfrau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jungfrau "Jungfrau") (4,158m or 13,642ft) [Eiger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eiger "Eiger") and [Mönch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%B6nch "Mönch") peaks, and its many picturesque valleys. In the southeast the long [Engadin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Engadin "Engadin") Valley, encompassing [St. Moritz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Moritz "St. Moritz"), is also well known; the highest peak in the neighbouring [Bernina Alps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bernina_Alps "Bernina Alps") is [Piz Bernina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Piz_Bernina "Piz Bernina") (4,049m or 13,284ft).[\[80\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Geo-87)

The Swiss Plateau has greater open and hilly landscapes, partly forested, partly open pastures, usually with grazing herds or vegetable and fruit fields, but it is still hilly. Large lakes and the biggest Swiss cities are found there.[\[80\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Geo-87)

Switzerland contains two small [enclaves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enclave "Enclave"): [Büsingen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B%C3%BCsingen "Büsingen") belongs to Germany, while [Campione d'Italia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campione_d'Italia "Campione d'Italia") belongs to Italy.[\[84\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-91) Switzerland has no exclaves.

### Climate

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Switzerland_K%C3%B6ppen.svg/500px-Switzerland_K%C3%B6ppen.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Switzerland_K%C3%B6ppen.svg) [Köppen–Geiger climate classification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C3%B6ppen_climate_classification "Köppen climate classification") map for Switzerland

The Swiss climate is generally [temperate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate "Temperate"), but can vary greatly across localities,[\[85\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Climate-92) from glacial conditions on the mountaintops to the near- [Mediterranean climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_climate "Mediterranean climate") at Switzerland's [southern tip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ticino "Ticino"). Some valley areas in the southern part of Switzerland offer cold-hardy palm trees, for example, the dwarf palm or the Chinese hemp palm. Summers tend to be warm and humid at times with periodic rainfall, ideal for pastures/grazing. The less humid winters in the mountains may see weeks-long intervals of stable conditions. At the same time, the lower lands tend to suffer from [inversion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inversion_(meteorology) "Inversion (meteorology)") during such periods, hiding the sun.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

A weather phenomenon known as the [föhn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%B6hn "Föhn") (with an identical effect to the [chinook wind](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinook_wind "Chinook wind")) can occur any time and is characterised by an unexpectedly warm wind, bringing low relative humidity air to the north of the Alps during rainfall periods south of the [main Alpine ridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Main_chain_of_the_Alps "Main chain of the Alps"). This works both ways across the alps but is more efficient if blowing from the south due to the steeper step for oncoming wind. Valleys running south to north trigger the strongest effect. The driest conditions persist in all inner alpine valleys that receive less rain because arriving clouds lose a lot of their moisture content while crossing the mountains before reaching these areas. Large alpine areas such as [Graubünden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graub%C3%BCnden "Graubünden") remain drier than pre-alpine areas, and as in the main valley of the [Valais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valais "Valais"), wine grapes are grown there.[\[86\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-FederalClimateMaps-93)

The wettest conditions persist in the [high Alps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_Alps "High Alps") and in the [Ticino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ticino "Ticino") canton, which has much sun yet heavy bursts of rain from time to time.[\[86\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-FederalClimateMaps-93) Precipitation tends to be spread moderately throughout the year, with a peak in summer. Autumn is the driest season, winter receives less precipitation than summer, yet the weather patterns in Switzerland are not in a stable climate system. They can vary from year to year with no strict and predictable periods.[\[87\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-94)[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-95)

### Climate change

[Global warming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_warming "Global warming") has changed the climate in Switzerland,[\[89\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-96)
and [extreme weather](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extreme_weather "Extreme weather") events, including heat waves and [heavy rainfall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heavy_rain "Heavy rain"), have become more frequent. Since the pre-industrial period of 1871–1900, the national average temperature has risen by 2.9°C, more than twice the global average.[\[90\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-97)
Since 1971, warming has increased continuously every decade.
The nationwide average annual temperature in 2022 reached 7.4°C (1.6°C above the [1991–2020 norm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climatological_normal "Climatological normal")), the highest value since measurements began in 1864.[\[91\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-98)
The nine warmest years in Switzerland were all recorded after 2010, with [2022 being the hottest year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_European_heatwaves "2022 European heatwaves"), followed by [2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_European_heatwaves "2023 European heatwaves"), [2024](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_European_heatwaves "2024 European heatwaves"), [2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_European_heatwave "2018 European heatwave") and [2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_droughts_and_heat_waves "2020 droughts and heat waves").

### Environment

Main articles: [Geography of Switzerland §Environment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Switzerland#Environment "Geography of Switzerland"), [Environmental movement in Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_movement_in_Switzerland "Environmental movement in Switzerland"), and [Health in Switzerland §Environmental issues related to health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Switzerland#Environmental_issues_related_to_health "Health in Switzerland")

Switzerland contains two terrestrial ecoregions: [Western European broadleaf forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_European_broadleaf_forests "Western European broadleaf forests") and [Alps conifer and mixed forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alps_conifer_and_mixed_forests "Alps conifer and mixed forests").[\[92\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-99)

Switzerland's many small valleys separated by high mountains often host unique ecologies. The mountainous regions themselves offer a rich range of plants not found at other altitudes. The climatic, geological and topographical conditions of the alpine region make for a fragile ecosystem that is particularly sensitive to [climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change "Climate change").[\[85\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Climate-92)[\[93\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-100) According to the [2014 Environmental Performance Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_Performance_Index "Environmental Performance Index"), Switzerland ranks first among 132 nations in safeguarding the environment, due to its high scores on environmental public health, its heavy reliance on renewable sources of energy ( [hydropower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydropower "Hydropower") and [geothermal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geothermal_energy "Geothermal energy") energy), and its level of [greenhouse gas emissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenhouse_gas_emissions "Greenhouse gas emissions").[\[94\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-101) In 2020 it was ranked third out of 180 countries.[\[95\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-102) The country pledged to cut [GHG emissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GHG_emissions "GHG emissions") by 50% by 2030 compared to the level of 1990 and plans to reach zero emissions by 2050.[\[96\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-103)

However, access to [biocapacity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biocapacity "Biocapacity") in Switzerland is far lower than the world average. In 2016, Switzerland had 1.0 hectares[\[97\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-GFN-104) of biocapacity per person within its territory, 40 per cent less than world average of 1.6. In contrast, in 2016, Swiss consumption required 4.6 hectares of biocapacity – their [ecological footprint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecological_footprint "Ecological footprint"), 4.6 times as much as Swiss territory can support. The remainder comes from other countries and the shared resources (such as the atmosphere impacted by greenhouse gas emissions).[\[97\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-GFN-104) Switzerland had a 2019 [Forest Landscape Integrity Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_Landscape_Integrity_Index "Forest Landscape Integrity Index") mean score of 3.53/10, ranking it 150th globally out of 172 countries.[\[98\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-105)

Switzerland ranked 9th in the [Environmental Performance Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_Performance_Index "Environmental Performance Index") for 2024.[\[99\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-106) It scored well in parameters including air pollution, sanitation and drinking water, waste management, and climate change mitigation.[\[100\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-107)

### Urbanisation

Main article: [Towns of Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cities_of_Switzerland "Cities of Switzerland")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Sion_de_la_Bisse_de_Clavau.jpg/500px-Sion_de_la_Bisse_de_Clavau.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Sion_de_la_Bisse_de_Clavau.jpg) Urbanisation in the [Rhone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhone "Rhone") Valley (outskirts of [Sion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sion,_Switzerland "Sion, Switzerland"))

About 85% of the population live in urban areas.[\[101\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-108)[\[102\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Cities-109) Switzerland went from a largely rural country to an urban one from 1930 to 2000. After 1935 urban development claimed as much of the Swiss landscape as it did during the prior 2,000 years. [Urban sprawl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_sprawl "Urban sprawl") affects the plateau, the Jura and the Alpine foothills,[\[103\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-110) raising concerns about land use.[\[104\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-111) During the 21st century, population growth in urban areas is higher than in the countryside.[\[102\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Cities-109)
