Switzerland has a dense network of complementary large, medium and small towns.[\[102\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Cities-109) The plateau is densely populated with about 400 people per km2 and the landscape shows uninterrupted signs of human presence.[\[105\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-112) The weight of the largest metropolitan areas – [Zurich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zurich "Zurich"), Geneva– [Lausanne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lausanne "Lausanne"), [Basel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basel "Basel") and [Bern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bern "Bern") – tend to increase.[\[102\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Cities-109)\[ _[clarification needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Please_clarify "Wikipedia:Please clarify")_\] The importance of these urban areas is greater than their population suggests.[\[102\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Cities-109) These urban centres are recognised for their high quality of life.[\[106\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-113)

The average population density in 2019 was 215.2 inhabitants per square kilometre (557/sqmi).[\[107\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-BFS-cantons2019-114):79 In the largest canton by area, [Graubünden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graub%C3%BCnden "Graubünden"), lying entirely in the Alps, population density falls to 28.0 inhabitants per square kilometre (73/sqmi).[\[107\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-BFS-cantons2019-114):30 In the [canton of Zurich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canton_of_Zurich "Canton of Zurich"), with its large urban capital, the density is 926.8 per square kilometre (2,400/sqmi).[\[107\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-BFS-cantons2019-114):76

## Government and politics

Main articles: [Politics of Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Switzerland "Politics of Switzerland") and [Elections in Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elections_in_Switzerland "Elections in Switzerland")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/Bundeshaus_Bern_2009%2C_Flooffy.jpg/500px-Bundeshaus_Bern_2009%2C_Flooffy.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bundeshaus_Bern_2009,_Flooffy.jpg) The [Federal Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Palace "Federal Palace"), seat of the [Federal Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Assembly_of_Switzerland "Federal Assembly of Switzerland") and the [Federal Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Federal_Council "Swiss Federal Council")

According to [International IDEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Institute_for_Democracy_and_Electoral_Assistance "International Institute for Democracy and Electoral Assistance")'s Global State of Democracy (GSoD) Indices and Democracy Tracker, Switzerland performs in the high range on overall democratic measures, with particular weaknesses in electoral participation.[\[108\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-115)[\[109\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-116)[\[110\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-117)

The [Federal Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Federal_Constitution "Swiss Federal Constitution") adopted in 1848 is the legal foundation of Switzerland's federal state.[\[111\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Politics-118) It is regularly modified by referendum and has been completely revised twice, in 1874 and 1999. The constitution outlines rights of individuals and citizen participation in public affairs, divides the powers between the confederation and the cantons and defines federal jurisdiction and authority. Three main bodies govern on the federal level: the [Federal Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Assembly_(Switzerland) "Federal Assembly (Switzerland)") (legislative), the [Federal Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Council_(Switzerland) "Federal Council (Switzerland)") (executive) and the [federal courts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_courts_of_Switzerland "Federal courts of Switzerland") (judicial).[\[112\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-119)

### Federal Assembly

The [Federal Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Assembly_(Switzerland) "Federal Assembly (Switzerland)") is the [parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament "Parliament") of Switzerland. It consists of two houses: The [Council of States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_States_(Switzerland) "Council of States (Switzerland)") has 46 members, two from each canton and one from each [half-canton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Half-canton "Half-canton"). The [National Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Council_(Switzerland) "National Council (Switzerland)") has 200 members, apportioned to reflect the population of each canton, and elected by [proportional representation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panachage "Panachage") within each canton. Members of both houses serve for four years and work part-time ( _Milizsystem_).[\[113\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-120) When both houses are in joint session, they are known collectively as the [Federal Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Assembly_of_Switzerland "Federal Assembly of Switzerland"). Through [optional referendums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optional_referendum "Optional referendum"), citizens may challenge any law passed by parliament.[\[111\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Politics-118)

### Federal Council

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Gruppenbild_Bundesrat_2026.jpg/500px-Gruppenbild_Bundesrat_2026.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Gruppenbild_Bundesrat_2026.jpg) The official photo of the [Swiss Federal Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Federal_Council "Swiss Federal Council") in 2026 (from left to right):

[Viktor Rossi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viktor_Rossi "Viktor Rossi") ( [Federal chancellor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chancellor_of_Switzerland "Chancellor of Switzerland"))

[Beat Jans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beat_Jans "Beat Jans")

[Albert Rösti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_R%C3%B6sti "Albert Rösti")

[Ignazio Cassis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ignazio_Cassis "Ignazio Cassis") ( [Vice president](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_Swiss_Confederation "President of the Swiss Confederation") in 2026)

[Guy Parmelin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guy_Parmelin "Guy Parmelin") ( [President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_Swiss_Confederation "President of the Swiss Confederation") in 2026)

[Karin Keller-Sutter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karin_Keller-Sutter "Karin Keller-Sutter")

[Elisabeth Baume-Schneider](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elisabeth_Baume-Schneider "Elisabeth Baume-Schneider")

[Martin Pfister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martin_Pfister "Martin Pfister")

The Federal Council is the Swiss executive. It leads the [federal administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_administration_of_Switzerland "Federal administration of Switzerland") and serves as a collective [head of state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Head_of_state "Head of state"). It is a [collegial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Collegial "Collegial") body of seven members, elected to four-year terms by the Federal Assembly, which oversees the council. Each member leads a department of the federal government. Each year, one member of the Council is elected [President of the Confederation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_Confederation "President of the Confederation") by the Assembly, with the presidency traditionally rotating between members. The President chairs and represents the government, but has [no additional powers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primus_inter_pares "Primus inter pares") and remains the head of their department.[\[111\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Politics-118)

The government has been a coalition of the four major political parties since 1959, each party having a number of seats that roughly reflects its share of the electorate and representation in the federal parliament. This distribution is called the " [magic formula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magic_formula_(Swiss_politics) "Magic formula (Swiss politics)")". Since 2003, the seven seats in the Federal Council have been distributed as follows:

- 1 seat for [The Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Centre_(political_party) "The Centre (political party)")[\[h\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-The_Centre-121)
- 2 seats for the [Free Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Democratic_Party_of_Switzerland "Free Democratic Party of Switzerland") (FDP/PRD)
- 2 seats for the [Social Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_Democratic_Party_of_Switzerland "Social Democratic Party of Switzerland") (SP/PS)
- 2 seats for the [Swiss People's Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_People's_Party "Swiss People's Party") (SVP/UDC)[\[i\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-BDP-122)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/Tribunal_Federal_-_wide.jpg/500px-Tribunal_Federal_-_wide.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Tribunal_Federal_-_wide.jpg) [Federal Supreme Court of Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Supreme_Court_of_Switzerland "Federal Supreme Court of Switzerland") building in [Lausanne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lausanne "Lausanne")

### Supreme Court

The function of the [Federal Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Supreme_Court_of_Switzerland "Federal Supreme Court of Switzerland") is to hear appeals against rulings of cantonal or federal courts. The judges are elected by the Federal Assembly for six-year terms.[\[114\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-123)

### Direct democracy

Main article: [Voting in Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_in_Switzerland "Voting in Switzerland")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/87/Landsgemeinde_Glarus_2006.jpg/500px-Landsgemeinde_Glarus_2006.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Landsgemeinde_Glarus_2006.jpg) The [Landsgemeinde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landsgemeinde "Landsgemeinde") is an old form of [direct democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_democracy "Direct democracy"), still in practice in two cantons.

[Direct democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Direct_democracy "Direct democracy") and [federalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federalism "Federalism") are hallmarks of the Swiss political system defined in the Swiss constitution.[\[115\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-CHPS-124) The Swiss people are subject to three legal jurisdictions: the municipal, cantonal and federal levels. At the federal level, popular rights[\[j\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-popular_rights-125) include the right to submit a federal initiative and a referendum, both of which may overturn parliamentary decisions.[\[116\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-126)[\[111\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Politics-118)[\[117\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-127)

There are three types of federal referendum in Switzerland:

- An [optional referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Optional_referendum "Optional referendum") challenges a law passed by parliament. It requires gathering 50,000 signatures or the objection of eight cantons within 100 days. Voters decide by a [simple majority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majority "Majority") whether to accept or reject the law.[\[111\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Politics-118)
- A [mandatory referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_referendums_in_Switzerland "Mandatory referendums in Switzerland") is required for all [constitutional amendments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_amendments "Constitutional amendments") passed by the Federal Assembly. Constitutional amendments require a [double majority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Double_majority "Double majority"), i.e. both a majority of the national popular vote and a majority of cantonal votes.[\[k\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-majority_cantonal_votes-128)[\[115\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-CHPS-124)
- A [popular initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_initiative_in_Switzerland "Popular initiative in Switzerland") is an amendment to the constitution proposed by citizens.[\[l\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-initiative_1999-129) A petition supporting the initiative must be signed by 100,000 voters within 18 months for a referendum to be held. Popular initiatives also require a double majority to pass.[\[118\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-130)

The Federal Council and the Federal Assembly can supplement a popular initiative with a counterproposal. Voters must then indicate their preference if both proposals are accepted.

### Cantons

Main article: [Cantons of Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantons_of_Switzerland "Cantons of Switzerland")

See also: [Spatial planning in Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spatial_planning_in_Switzerland "Spatial planning in Switzerland")

The Swiss Confederation consists of 26 cantons.[\[111\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Politics-118)[\[119\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-CHAuthorities-131) The cantons are [federated states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federated_state "Federated state"), with permanent constitutional status and a high degree of independence relative to the [subnational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_division "Administrative division") divisions of most countries. Under the Federal Constitution, all 26 cantons are equal in status, except that six (referred to often as the [half-cantons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Half-cantons "Half-cantons")) are represented by one councillor instead of two in the [Council of States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Council_of_States "Swiss Council of States") and have only half a cantonal vote with respect to the required cantonal majority in [referendums on constitutional amendments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Voting_in_Switzerland#Constitutional_referendums_(Popular_initiatives) "Voting in Switzerland"). Each canton has its own constitution and its own parliament, government, police and courts.[\[119\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-CHAuthorities-131) However, considerable differences define the individual cantons, particularly in terms of population and geographical area. Their populations vary between 16,003 (Appenzell Innerrhoden) and 1,487,969 (Zurich), and their area between 37km2 (14sqmi) (Basel-Stadt) and 7,105km2 (2,743sqmi) ( [Grisons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grisons "Grisons")).

#### Municipalities

Main article: [Municipalities of Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipalities_of_Switzerland "Municipalities of Switzerland")

As of 2026 the cantons comprised 2,110 municipalities.[\[120\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-132)

### Federal City

Until 1848, the loosely coupled Confederation did not have a central political organisation. Issues thought to affect the whole Confederation were the subject of periodic meetings in various locations.[\[121\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-133)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/Bern_007_%2835250800705%29.jpg/500px-Bern_007_%2835250800705%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bern_007_(35250800705).jpg) The [Old City of Bern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_City_of_Bern "Old City of Bern")

In 1848, the federal constitution provided that details concerning federal institutions, such as their locations, should be addressed by the [Federal Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Assembly_of_Switzerland "Federal Assembly of Switzerland") (BV 1848 Art. 108). Thus on 28 November 1848, the Federal Assembly voted in the majority to locate the seat of government in Bern and, as a prototypical federal compromise, to assign other federal institutions, such as the [Federal Polytechnical School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ETH_Zurich "ETH Zurich") (1854, the later ETH) to Zurich, and other institutions to Lucerne, such as the later [SUVA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SUVA "SUVA") (1912) and the Federal Insurance Court (1917).[\[2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Bundesstadt-3) Other federal institutions were subsequently attributed to [Lausanne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lausanne "Lausanne") ( [Federal Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Supreme_Court_of_Switzerland "Federal Supreme Court of Switzerland") in 1872, and [EPFL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EPFL "EPFL") in 1969), [Bellinzona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bellinzona "Bellinzona") ( [Federal Criminal Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Criminal_Court_of_Switzerland "Federal Criminal Court of Switzerland"), 2004), and [St. Gallen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Gallen "St. Gallen") ( [Federal Administrative Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Administrative_Court_(Switzerland) "Federal Administrative Court (Switzerland)") and [Federal Patent Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Patent_Court_(Switzerland) "Federal Patent Court (Switzerland)"), 2012).

The 1999 Constitution does not mention a Federal City, and the Federal Council has yet to address the matter.[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-134) Thus no city in Switzerland has the _official_ status either of capital or of Federal City. Nevertheless, Bern is commonly referred to as "Federal City" (German: _Bundesstadt_, French: _ville fédérale_, [Italian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_language "Italian language"): _città federale_). However the largest city, Zurich, is used when [selecting a time zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tz_database_time_zones "List of tz database time zones") on [computer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Computer "Computer") systems.

### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Switzerland "Foreign relations of Switzerland")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d8/United_Nations_Flags_-_cropped.jpg/500px-United_Nations_Flags_-_cropped.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:United_Nations_Flags_-_cropped.jpg) The [Palace of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_of_Nations "Palace of Nations"), the European headquarters of the [United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Office_at_Geneva "United Nations Office at Geneva") in Geneva

Traditionally, Switzerland avoids alliances that might entail military, political, or direct economic action and has been neutral since the end of [its expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Growth_of_the_Old_Swiss_Confederacy "Growth of the Old Swiss Confederacy") in 1515. Its [policy of neutrality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_neutrality "Swiss neutrality") was internationally recognised at the [Congress of Vienna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_of_Vienna "Congress of Vienna") in 1815.[\[123\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Neutrality-135)[\[124\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-136) Swiss neutrality has been [questioned at times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_neutrality#Criticism "Swiss neutrality").[\[125\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-137)[\[126\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-138) In 2002 Switzerland became a full member of the United Nations.[\[123\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Neutrality-135) It was the first state to join it by [referendum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2002_Swiss_referendums "2002 Swiss referendums"). Switzerland maintains diplomatic relations with almost all countries and historically has served as an intermediary between other states.[\[123\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Neutrality-135) Switzerland is not a member of the [European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union "European Union"); the Swiss people have consistently rejected membership since the early 1990s.[\[123\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Neutrality-135) However, Switzerland does participate in the [Schengen Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schengen_Area "Schengen Area").[\[127\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-139)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1a/Flag_of_the_Red_Cross.svg/500px-Flag_of_the_Red_Cross.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Flag_of_the_Red_Cross.svg) The colour-reversed Swiss flag became the symbol of the Red Cross Movement, founded in 1863 by [Henry Dunant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Dunant "Henry Dunant").[\[128\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-140)[\[129\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-141)

Many international institutions have headquarters in Switzerland, in part because of its policy of neutrality. [Geneva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva "Geneva") is the birthplace of the [Red Cross and Red Crescent Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Cross_and_Red_Crescent_Movement "Red Cross and Red Crescent Movement"), the [Geneva Conventions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_Conventions "Geneva Conventions") and, since 2006, hosts the [United Nations Human Rights Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Human_Rights_Council "United Nations Human Rights Council"). Even though Switzerland is one of the most recent countries to join the United Nations, the [Palace of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_of_Nations "Palace of Nations") in Geneva is the second biggest centre for the United Nations after the [headquarters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Headquarters_of_the_United_Nations "Headquarters of the United Nations") in New York. Switzerland was a founding member and hosted the [League of Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/League_of_Nations "League of Nations").[\[62\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-:0-69)

Apart from the United Nations headquarters, the Swiss Confederation is host to many UN agencies, including the [World Health Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Health_Organization "World Health Organization") (WHO), the [International Labour Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Labour_Organization "International Labour Organization") (ILO), the [International Telecommunication Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Telecommunication_Union "International Telecommunication Union") (ITU), the [United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_High_Commissioner_for_Refugees "United Nations High Commissioner for Refugees") (UNHCR) and about 200 other international organisations, including the [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization "World Trade Organization") and the [World Intellectual Property Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Intellectual_Property_Organization "World Intellectual Property Organization").[\[123\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Neutrality-135) The annual meetings of the [World Economic Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Economic_Forum "World Economic Forum") in [Davos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davos "Davos") bring together business and political leaders from Switzerland and foreign countries to discuss important issues. The headquarters of the [Bank for International Settlements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bank_for_International_Settlements "Bank for International Settlements") (BIS) moved to [Basel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basel "Basel") in 1930.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

Many sports federations and organisations are located in the country, including the [International Handball Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Handball_Federation "International Handball Federation") in Basel, the [International Basketball Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Basketball_Federation "International Basketball Federation") in Geneva, the [Union of European Football Associations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA "UEFA") (UEFA) in [Nyon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nyon "Nyon"), the [International Federation of Association Football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIFA "FIFA") (FIFA) and the [International Ice Hockey Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Ice_Hockey_Federation "International Ice Hockey Federation") both in [Zurich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zurich "Zurich"), the [International Cycling Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Cycling_Union "International Cycling Union") in [Aigle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aigle "Aigle"), and the [International Olympic Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Olympic_Committee "International Olympic Committee") in [Lausanne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lausanne "Lausanne").[\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-142)

Switzerland became a member of the [United Nations Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council "United Nations Security Council") for the 2023–2024 period.[\[131\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-143) According to the 2024 [Global Peace Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Peace_Index "Global Peace Index"), Switzerland is the 6th most peaceful country in the world.[\[132\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-144)

#### Switzerland and the European Union

Further information: [Switzerland–European Union relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland%E2%80%93European_Union_relations "Switzerland–European Union relations")

Although not a member, Switzerland maintains relationships with the EU and European countries through bilateral agreements. The Swiss have brought their economic practices largely into conformity with those of the EU, in an effort to compete internationally. [EU membership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Future_enlargement_of_the_European_Union#States_not_on_the_agenda "Future enlargement of the European Union") faces considerable negative popular sentiment. It is opposed by the conservative [SVP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_People's_Party "Swiss People's Party") party, the largest party in the National Council, and not advocated by several other political parties. The membership application was formally withdrawn in 2016. The western French-speaking areas and the urban regions of the rest of the country tend to be more pro-EU, but do not form a significant share of the population.[\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-145)[\[134\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-146)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ad/EU_and_EFTA.svg/500px-EU_and_EFTA.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:EU_and_EFTA.svg) Members of the [European Free Trade Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Free_Trade_Association "European Free Trade Association") (green) participate in the [European single market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_single_market "European single market") and are part of the [Schengen Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schengen_Area "Schengen Area").

An Integration Office operates under the [Department of Foreign Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Department_of_Foreign_Affairs "Federal Department of Foreign Affairs") and the [Department of Economic Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Department_of_Economic_Affairs "Federal Department of Economic Affairs"). Seven bilateral agreements liberalised trade ties, taking effect in 2001. This first series of bilateral agreements included the free movement of persons. A second series of agreements covering nine areas was signed in 2004, including the [Schengen Treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schengen_Treaty "Schengen Treaty") and the [Dublin Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dublin_Convention "Dublin Convention").[\[135\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-147)

In 2006, a referendum approved 1 billion francs of supportive investment in Southern and Central European countries in support of positive ties to the EU as a whole. A further referendum will be needed to approve 300 million francs to support Romania and Bulgaria and their recent admission.

The Swiss have faced EU and international pressure to reduce [banking secrecy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banking_secrecy "Banking secrecy") and raise tax rates to parity with the EU. Preparatory discussions involved four areas: the electricity market, participation in project [Galileo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galileo_positioning_system "Galileo positioning system"), cooperating with the [European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Centre_for_Disease_Prevention_and_Control "European Centre for Disease Prevention and Control") and certificates of origin for food products.[\[136\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-148)\[ _[needs update](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual_of_Style/Dates_and_numbers#Chronological_items "Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Dates and numbers")_\]

Switzerland is a member of the Schengen passport-free zone. Land [border checkpoints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Border_checkpoint "Border checkpoint") monitor goods movements, but not people.[\[137\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-149)

### Military

Main articles: [Swiss Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Armed_Forces "Swiss Armed Forces") and [Pontifical Swiss Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontifical_Swiss_Guard "Pontifical Swiss Guard")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8e/Switzerland_-_Air_Force_McDonnell_Douglas_FA-18C_Hornet_-_cropped.jpg/500px-Switzerland_-_Air_Force_McDonnell_Douglas_FA-18C_Hornet_-_cropped.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Switzerland_-_Air_Force_McDonnell_Douglas_FA-18C_Hornet_-_cropped.jpg) A Swiss Air Force [F/A-18 Hornet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F/A-18_Hornet "F/A-18 Hornet") at [Axalp Air Show](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axalp_Air_Show "Axalp Air Show")

The [Swiss Armed Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Armed_Forces "Swiss Armed Forces"), including the [Land Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ground_Forces "Ground Forces") and the [Air Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Air_Force "Swiss Air Force"), are [composed mostly of conscripts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conscription_in_Switzerland "Conscription in Switzerland"), male citizens aged from 20 to 34 (in exceptional cases up to 50) years. Being a [landlocked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landlocked "Landlocked") country, Switzerland has no navy; however, on lakes bordering neighbouring countries, armed boats patrol. Swiss citizens are prohibited from serving in foreign armies, except for the [Swiss Guards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontifical_Swiss_Guard "Pontifical Swiss Guard") of the [Vatican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vatican_City "Vatican City"), or if they are [dual citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dual_citizen "Dual citizen") of a foreign country and reside there.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

The Swiss militia system stipulates that soldiers keep their army-issued equipment, including [fully automatic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fully_automatic "Fully automatic") personal weapons, at home.[\[138\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-150) Women can serve voluntarily. Men usually receive military conscription orders for training at the age of 18.[\[139\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-151) About two-thirds of young Swiss are found suitable for service; for the others, various forms of alternative service are available.[\[140\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-152) Annually, approximately 20,000 persons are trained in recruit centres for 18 to 21 weeks. The reform "Army XXI" was adopted by popular vote in 2003, replacing "Army 95", reducing the rolls from 400,000 to about 200,000. Of those, 120,000 are active in periodic Army training, and 80,000 are non-training reserves.[\[141\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-153)

The newest reform of the military, _Weiterentwicklung der Armee_ (WEA; English: Further development of the Army), started in 2018 and was expected to reduce the number of army personnel to 100,000 by the end of 2022.[\[142\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-154)[\[143\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-155)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/45/SKdt-Fahrzeug_-_Schweizer_Armee_-_Steel_Parade_2006.jpg/500px-SKdt-Fahrzeug_-_Schweizer_Armee_-_Steel_Parade_2006.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:SKdt-Fahrzeug_-_Schweizer_Armee_-_Steel_Parade_2006.jpg) Swiss-built [Mowag Eagles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mowag_Eagle "Mowag Eagle") of the land forces

Overall, three general mobilisations have been declared to ensure the integrity and neutrality of Switzerland. The first mobilisation was held in response to the [Franco-Prussian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco-Prussian_War "Franco-Prussian War") of 1870–71; while the second was in response to the [First World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_World_War "First World War") outbreak in August 1914; the third mobilisation took place in September 1939 in response to the [German attack on Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_attack_on_Poland "German attack on Poland").[\[144\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-156)

Because of its neutrality policy, the Swiss army does not take part in armed conflicts in other countries but joins some peacekeeping missions. Since 2000 the armed force department has maintained the [Onyx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Onyx_(interception_system) "Onyx (interception system)") intelligence gathering system to monitor satellite communications.[\[145\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-157)

[Gun politics in Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gun_politics_in_Switzerland "Gun politics in Switzerland") are unique in Europe in that 2–3.5million guns are in the hands of civilians, giving the nation an estimate of 28–41 guns per 100 people.[\[146\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-158) As per the Small Arms Survey, only 324,484 guns are owned by the military.[\[147\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-159) Only 143,372 are in the hands of soldiers.[\[148\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-160) However, ammunition is no longer issued.[\[149\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-161)[\[150\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-162)

## Economy

Main articles: [Economy of Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Switzerland "Economy of Switzerland") and [Economic history of Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_Switzerland "Economic history of Switzerland")

See also: [Banking in Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banking_in_Switzerland "Banking in Switzerland") and [Federal budget of Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_budget_of_Switzerland "Federal budget of Switzerland")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/Aerial_view_of_Zurich.JPG/500px-Aerial_view_of_Zurich.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Aerial_view_of_Zurich.JPG) The Greater [Zurich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zurich "Zurich") Area, home to 1.5million inhabitants and 150,000 companies, is one of the most important economic centres in the world.[\[151\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-163)

Switzerland has a stable, prosperous and [high-tech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-tech "High-tech") economy. It is frequently ranked among the world's wealthiest country per capita; for example in 2024 the [CIA world factbook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CIA_world_factbook "CIA world factbook") ranked Switzerland as having the 10th highest [GDP per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GDP_per_capita "GDP per capita") (at [purchasing power parity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity "Purchasing power parity")) in the world.[\[152\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-164) The country ranks as one of the [least corrupt countries in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_Perceptions_Index "Corruption Perceptions Index"),[\[153\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-165)[\[154\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-166)[\[155\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-167) while [its banking sector](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banking_in_Switzerland "Banking in Switzerland") was rated in 2018 as " [one of the most corrupt in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_in_Switzerland#Banking "Corruption in Switzerland")".[\[156\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-168) It has the world's [twentieth largest economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_(nominal) "List of countries by GDP (nominal)") by nominal GDP and the [thirty-eighth largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_(PPP) "List of countries by GDP (PPP)") by purchasing power parity. As of 2021, it is the [thirteenth largest exporter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_exports "List of countries by exports"), and the [fifth largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_exports_per_capita "List of countries by exports per capita") per capita. Zurich and Geneva are regarded as [global cities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_cities "Global cities"), ranked as [Alpha and Beta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Globalization_and_World_Cities_Research_Network "Globalization and World Cities Research Network") respectively. Basel is the capital of Switzerland's pharmaceutical industry, hosting [Novartis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novartis "Novartis"), [Roche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roche "Roche"), and many other players. It is one of the world's most important centres for the life sciences industry.[\[157\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-169)

Switzerland had the second-highest global rating in the [Index of Economic Freedom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Index_of_Economic_Freedom "Index of Economic Freedom") 2023,[\[158\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-170) while also providing significant public services.[\[159\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-171) On a per capita basis, nominal GDP is higher than those of the larger Western and Central European economies and Japan,[\[160\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-172) while [adjusted for purchasing power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_(PPP)_per_capita "List of countries by GDP (PPP) per capita"), Switzerland ranked 11th in 2017, fifth in 2018, and ninth in 2020.[\[161\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-173)

Origin of the capital at the 30 biggest Swiss corporations, 2018:[\[162\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-174)[\[m\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Rothschilds-175)

1. Switzerland (39.0%)
2. North America (33.0%)
3. Europe (24.0%)
4. Rest of the world (4.00%)

The [European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union "European Union") labelled Switzerland Europe's most innovative country.[\[163\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-176) Switzerland has been ranked the most innovative country in the [Global Innovation Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Innovation_Index "Global Innovation Index") in 2025, as it had done in 2024, 2023, 2022, 2021, 2020 and 2019.[\[164\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-177)[\[165\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-178)[\[166\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-179)[\[167\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-180)[\[168\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-181) It ranked 20th of 189 countries in the [Ease of Doing Business Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ease_of_Doing_Business_Index "Ease of Doing Business Index"). Switzerland's slow growth in the 1990s and the early 2000s increased support for economic reforms and harmonisation with the European Union.[\[169\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-economicsurvey2007-182)[\[170\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-183) In 2020, [IMD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Institute_for_Management_Development "International Institute for Management Development") placed Switzerland first in attracting skilled workers.[\[171\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-184)

For much of the 20th century, Switzerland was the wealthiest country in Europe by a considerable margin (per capita GDP).[\[172\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-185) Switzerland has one of the world's largest [account balances as a percentage of GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_current_account_balance_as_a_percentage_of_GDP "List of countries by current account balance as a percentage of GDP").[\[173\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-SWI-Are_most-186) In 2018, the canton of Basel-City had the highest GDP per capita, ahead of Zug and Geneva.[\[174\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-187) According to [Credit Suisse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Credit_Suisse "Credit Suisse"), only about 37% of residents own their own homes, one of the lowest rates of [home ownership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Home_ownership "Home ownership") in Europe. Housing and food price levels were 171% and 145% of the [EU-25](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EU-25 "EU-25") index in 2007, compared to 113% and 104% in Germany.[\[175\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-yearbook2008-188)

Switzerland is home to several large multinational corporations. The largest by revenue are [Glencore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glencore "Glencore"), [Gunvor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gunvor "Gunvor"), [Nestlé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nestl%C3%A9 "Nestlé"), [Mediterranean Shipping Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mediterranean_Shipping_Company "Mediterranean Shipping Company"), [Novartis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novartis "Novartis"), [Hoffmann-La Roche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoffmann-La_Roche "Hoffmann-La Roche"), [ABB](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABB "ABB"), [Mercuria Energy Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercuria_Energy_Group "Mercuria Energy Group") and [Adecco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adecco "Adecco").[\[176\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-189) Also, notable are [UBS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UBS "UBS"), [Zurich Insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zurich_Insurance "Zurich Insurance"), [Richemont](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richemont "Richemont"), [Credit Suisse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Credit_Suisse "Credit Suisse"), [Barry Callebaut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barry_Callebaut "Barry Callebaut"), [Swiss Re](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Re "Swiss Re"), [Rolex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rolex "Rolex"), [Tetra Pak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tetra_Pak "Tetra Pak"), [Swatch Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swatch_Group "Swatch Group") and [Swiss International Air Lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_International_Air_Lines "Swiss International Air Lines").

Switzerland's most important economic sector is manufacturing. Manufactured products include speciality [chemicals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chemical_industry "Chemical industry"), [health and pharmaceutical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharmaceutical_industry_in_Switzerland "Pharmaceutical industry in Switzerland") goods, scientific and precision [measuring instruments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Measuring_instrument "Measuring instrument") and [musical instruments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musical_instrument "Musical instrument"). The largest exported goods are chemicals (34% of exported goods), machines/electronics (20.9%), and precision instruments/ [watches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_watchmaking_industry "Swiss watchmaking industry") (16.9%).[\[175\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-yearbook2008-188) The service sector – especially banking and insurance, [commodities trading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Switzerland#Commodities_trading "Economy of Switzerland"), [tourism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Switzerland "Tourism in Switzerland"), and [international organisations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_organisations "International organisations") – is another important industry for Switzerland. Exported services amount to a third of exports.[\[175\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-yearbook2008-188)

[Agricultural protectionism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Switzerland "Agriculture in Switzerland")—a rare exception to Switzerland's free trade policies—contributes to high [food prices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_prices "Food prices"). Product market liberalisation is lagging behind many [EU countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EU_countries "EU countries") according to the [OECD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OECD "OECD").[\[169\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-economicsurvey2007-182) Apart from agriculture, economic and trade barriers between the European Union and Switzerland are minimal, and Switzerland has free trade agreements with many countries. Switzerland is a member of the [European Free Trade Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Free_Trade_Association "European Free Trade Association") (EFTA).

Switzerland is considered as the "land of [Cooperatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooperative "Cooperative")" with the ten largest cooperative companies accounting for more than 11% of GDP in 2018. They include [Migros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Migros "Migros") and [Coop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coop_(Switzerland) "Coop (Switzerland)"), the two largest retail companies in Switzerland.[\[177\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-190)

### Taxation and government spending

See also: [Taxation in Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxation_in_Switzerland "Taxation in Switzerland")

Switzerland is a [tax haven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_haven "Tax haven").[\[178\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-191) The private sector economy dominates. It features low tax rates; [tax revenue to GDP ratio is one of the smallest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_tax_revenue_to_GDP_ratio "List of countries by tax revenue to GDP ratio") of [developed countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Developed_countries "Developed countries"). The [Swiss Federal budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_budget_of_Switzerland "Federal budget of Switzerland") reached 62.8 billion Swiss francs in 2010, 11.35% of GDP; however, canton and municipality budgets are not counted as part of the federal budget. Total [government spending](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_spending "Government spending") is closer to 33.8% of GDP. The main sources of income for the federal government are the [value-added tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Value-added_tax "Value-added tax") (33% of tax revenue) and the direct federal tax (29%). The main areas of expenditure are in social welfare and finance/taxes. The expenditures of the Swiss Confederation have been growing from 7% of GDP in 1960 to 9.7% in 1990 and 10.7% in 2010. While the social welfare and finance sectors and tax grew from 35% in 1990 to 48.2% in 2010, a significant reduction of expenditures has been occurring in agriculture and national defence; from 26.5% to 12.4% (estimation for the year 2015).[\[179\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-192)[\[180\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-193)

### Labour force

See also: [Immigration to Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_Switzerland "Immigration to Switzerland")

Slightly more than 5 million people work in Switzerland;[\[181\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-194) about 25% of employees belonged to a trade union in 2004.[\[182\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-195) Switzerland has a more flexible [labour market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Labor_market "Labor market") than neighbouring countries and the unemployment rate is consistently low.[\[183\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-196) The unemployment rate increased from 1.7% in June 2000 to 4.4% in December 2009.[\[184\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-197) It then decreased to 3.2% in 2014 and held steady for several years, before further dropping to 2.5% in 2018 and 2.3% in 2019; in 2023 it had reached a 20-year low of 2%.[\[185\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-198) Population growth (from net immigration) reached 0.52% of population in 2004, increased in the following years before falling to 0.54% again in 2017.[\[175\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-yearbook2008-188)[\[186\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-199) The [foreign citizen population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_immigrant_population "List of countries by immigrant population") was 28.9% in 2015, about the same as in Australia.[\[187\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-200)

In 2022, the median monthly gross income in Switzerland was 6,788 francs per month (equivalent to US$7,467 per month).[\[188\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-201)[\[173\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-SWI-Are_most-186) After rent, taxes and pension contributions, plus spending on goods and services, the average household has about 15% of its gross income left for savings. Though 61% of the population made less than the mean income, income inequality is relatively low with a [Gini coefficient](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gini_coefficient "Gini coefficient") of 29.7, placing Switzerland among the top 20 countries. In 2015, the richest 1% owned 35% of the wealth.[\[189\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-202) Wealth inequality increased through 2019.[\[190\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-203)

About 8.2% of the population live below [the national poverty line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_in_Switzerland "Poverty in Switzerland"), defined in Switzerland as earning less than CHF 3,990 per month for a household of two adults and two children, and a further 15% are at risk of poverty. Single-parent families, those with no post-compulsory education and those out of work are among the most likely to live below the poverty line. Although work is considered a way out of poverty, some 4.3% are considered working poor. One in ten jobs in Switzerland is considered low-paid; roughly 12% of Swiss workers hold such jobs, many of them women and foreigners.[\[173\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-SWI-Are_most-186)

### Science

Main article: [Science and technology in Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_and_technology_in_Switzerland "Science and technology in Switzerland")

See also: [List of Swiss Nobel laureates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Swiss_Nobel_laureates "List of Swiss Nobel laureates")

Switzerland is among the [countries with the highest number of Nobel laureates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Nobel_laureates_by_country "List of Nobel laureates by country"), both in total and per capita; of the 28 Swiss nationals who have won the [Nobel Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nobel_Prize "Nobel Prize"), 23 were recognised in the sciences. Among the most famous is [Albert Einstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Einstein "Albert Einstein"),[\[191\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-204) who became a Swiss citizen in 1901 and developed his theory of [special relativity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_relativity "Special relativity") in Bern. Among the Nobel laureates born or naturalised in Switzerland are [Vladimir Prelog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Prelog "Vladimir Prelog"), [Heinrich Rohrer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heinrich_Rohrer "Heinrich Rohrer"), [Richard Ernst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_R._Ernst "Richard R. Ernst"), [Edmond Fischer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edmond_Fischer "Edmond Fischer"), [Rolf Zinkernagel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rolf_Zinkernagel "Rolf Zinkernagel"), [Kurt Wüthrich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kurt_W%C3%BCthrich "Kurt Wüthrich") and [Jacques Dubochet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Dubochet "Jacques Dubochet"). Over 100 laureates across all fields have a relationship to Switzerland.[\[192\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-205)[\[n\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-nobel_prize-206) The [Nobel Peace Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nobel_Peace_Prize "Nobel Peace Prize") has been awarded nine times to organisations headquartered in Switzerland.[\[193\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-207)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/LHC%2C_CERN.jpg/500px-LHC%2C_CERN.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:LHC,_CERN.jpg) The [LHC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Large_Hadron_Collider "Large Hadron Collider") tunnel. CERN is the world's largest laboratory and also the birthplace of the [World Wide Web](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Wide_Web "World Wide Web").[\[194\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-208)

Geneva and the nearby French department of [Ain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ain "Ain") co-host the world's largest laboratory, [CERN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CERN "CERN"),[\[195\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-209) dedicated to [particle physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Particle_physics "Particle physics") research. Another important research centre is the [Paul Scherrer Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Scherrer_Institute "Paul Scherrer Institute"), which conducts [multi-disciplinary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-disciplinary "Multi-disciplinary") [research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Research_institute "Research institute") in the [natural](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_science "Natural science") and [engineering sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Engineering_sciences "Engineering sciences").

Notable Swiss inventions include [lysergic acid diethylamide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lysergic_acid_diethylamide "Lysergic acid diethylamide") (LSD), [diazepam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diazepam "Diazepam") (Valium), [Velcro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Velcro "Velcro"), and the [scanning tunnelling microscope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scanning_tunnelling_microscope "Scanning tunnelling microscope"), which earned inventors [Gerd Binnig](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerd_Binnig "Gerd Binnig") and [Heinrich Rohrer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heinrich_Rohrer "Heinrich Rohrer") the 1986 [Nobel Prize in Physics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nobel_Prize_in_Physics "Nobel Prize in Physics"). [Auguste Piccard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auguste_Piccard "Auguste Piccard") became the first person to enter the [Stratosphere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stratosphere "Stratosphere") with his pressurised hydrogen ballon, while his son [Jacques Piccard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacques_Piccard "Jacques Piccard") became one of the first people to explore the deepest known part of the world's [ocean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ocean "Ocean") (along with American [Don Walsh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don_Walsh "Don Walsh")).

The [Swiss Space Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Space_Office "Swiss Space Office") has been involved in various space technologies and programmes. It was one of the 10 founders of the [European Space Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Space_Agency "European Space Agency") in 1975 and is the seventh largest contributor to the ESA budget. In the private sector, several companies participate in the space industry, such as [Oerlikon Space](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oerlikon_Space "Oerlikon Space")[\[196\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-210) and Maxon Motors.[\[197\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-211)

### Energy

See also: [Energy in Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_in_Switzerland "Energy in Switzerland")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/66/Mauvoisin_Dam.jpg/960px-Mauvoisin_Dam.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Mauvoisin_Dam.jpg) Switzerland has the [tallest dams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_dams_in_the_world "List of tallest dams in the world") in Europe, among which the [Mauvoisin Dam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mauvoisin_Dam "Mauvoisin Dam"), in the Alps. Hydroelectric power is the most important domestic source of energy in the country.

Electricity generated in Switzerland is 56% from [hydroelectricity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroelectricity "Hydroelectricity") and 39% from [nuclear power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_power "Nuclear power"), producing negligible CO2. On 18 May 2003, two [anti-nuclear](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-nuclear "Anti-nuclear") referendums were defeated: _Moratorium Plus_, aimed at forbidding the building of new [nuclear power plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_power_plants "Nuclear power plants") (41.6% supported),[\[198\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-212) and Electricity Without Nuclear (33.7% supported) after a moratorium expired in 2000.[\[199\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-213) After the [Fukushima nuclear disaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fukushima_nuclear_disaster "Fukushima nuclear disaster"), in 2011 the government announced plans to end the use of nuclear energy in the following 20 to 30 years.[\[200\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-214) In November 2016, Swiss voters rejected a [Green Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Party_of_Switzerland "Green Party of Switzerland") referendum to accelerate the phaseout of nuclear power (45.8% supported).[\[201\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-215) The Swiss Federal Office of Energy (SFOE) is responsible for energy supply and energy use within the [Federal Department of Environment, Transport, Energy and Communications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Department_of_Environment,_Transport,_Energy_and_Communications "Federal Department of Environment, Transport, Energy and Communications") (DETEC). The agency supports the [2000-watt society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000-watt_society "2000-watt society") initiative to cut the nation's energy use by more than half by 2050.[\[202\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-216)

### Transport

Main article: [Transport in Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Switzerland "Transport in Switzerland")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/L%C3%B6tschberg_Tunnel.jpg/500px-L%C3%B6tschberg_Tunnel.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:L%C3%B6tschberg_Tunnel.jpg) Entrance of the new [Lötschberg Base Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%B6tschberg_Base_Tunnel "Lötschberg Base Tunnel"), the third-longest railway tunnel in the world, under the old [Lötschberg railway line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%B6tschberg_railway_line "Lötschberg railway line"). It was the first completed tunnel of the greater project [NRLA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NRLA "NRLA").

Switzerland had approximately 6,562,600 motor vehicles (excluding mopeds) in 2025.[\[203\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-l046-217) The largest category was passenger cars with 4,829,500 units (58.7% running on petrol, 24% on diesel, 5.2% on electricity and 11.9% hybrid).[\[203\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-l046-217)

The densest [rail network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rail_transport_in_Switzerland "Rail transport in Switzerland") in Europe spans 5,250 kilometres (3,260mi) and carried 614 million passengers in 2023.[\[204\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-218)[\[205\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-219) In 2023, each Swiss resident travelled on average 2,446 kilometres (1,520mi) by rail, more than any other European country.[\[206\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-220)[\[207\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-FSO-2016-221)\[ _[verification needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability "Wikipedia:Verifiability")_\] Virtually 100% of the network is electrified. 60% of the network is operated by the [Swiss Federal Railways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Federal_Railways "Swiss Federal Railways") (SBBCFFFFS). Besides the second largest [standard gauge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_gauge "Standard gauge") railway company, [BLS AG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BLS_AG "BLS AG"), two railways companies operate on [narrow gauge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narrow_gauge "Narrow gauge") networks: the [Rhaetian Railway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhaetian_Railway "Rhaetian Railway") (RhB) in Graubünden, which includes some World Heritage lines,[\[208\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-222) and the [Matterhorn Gotthard Bahn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matterhorn_Gotthard_Bahn "Matterhorn Gotthard Bahn") (MGB), which co-operates with RhB the [Glacier Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glacier_Express "Glacier Express") between [Zermatt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zermatt "Zermatt") and [St. Moritz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Moritz "St. Moritz")/ [Davos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davos "Davos"). Switzerland operates the [world's longest and deepest railway tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_longest_tunnels "List of longest tunnels") and the first flat, low-level route through the Alps, the 57.1-kilometre-long (35.5mi) [Gotthard Base Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gotthard_Base_Tunnel "Gotthard Base Tunnel"), the largest part of the [New Railway Link through the Alps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NRLA "NRLA") (NRLA) project.

[Public transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_transport "Public transport") is very popular. For example, a 2010 microcensus discovered that in [Zurich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zurich "Zurich"), Switzerland's largest city, 32% of its inhabitants use [the city's public transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_transport_in_Zurich "Public transport in Zurich") regularly (trams or trolleybuses, of which 60% used at least those two modes), while 26% depended on a personal vehicle. Fewer than half the residents owned a car or a motorcycle.[\[209\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-223)

Switzerland has a publicly managed, toll-free road network financed by highway permits as well as vehicle and petrol taxes. The Swiss autobahn/autoroute system requires the annual purchase of a [vignette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vignette_(road_tax) "Vignette (road tax)") (toll sticker)—for 40 [Swiss francs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_franc "Swiss franc")—to use its roadways, including passenger cars and trucks. The Swiss autobahn/autoroute network stretches for 2,259km (1,404mi) and has one of the highest motorway densities in the world.[\[210\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-224)[\[211\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-225)

[Zurich Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zurich_Airport "Zurich Airport") is Switzerland's largest international flight gateway; it handled 31.2million passengers in 2024.[\[212\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-226) The other international airports are [Geneva Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_Airport "Geneva Airport") (13.9million passengers in 2012),[\[213\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-227) [EuroAirport Basel Mulhouse Freiburg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EuroAirport_Basel_Mulhouse_Freiburg "EuroAirport Basel Mulhouse Freiburg") (located in France), [Bern Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bern_Airport "Bern Airport"), [Lugano Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lugano_Airport "Lugano Airport"), [St. Gallen-Altenrhein Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Gallen-Altenrhein_Airport "St. Gallen-Altenrhein Airport") and [Sion Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sion_Airport "Sion Airport"). [Swiss International Air Lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_International_Air_Lines "Swiss International Air Lines") is the flag carrier. Its main hub is Zurich, but it is legally domiciled in Basel.

In November 2025, Switzerland's Federal Roads Office (FEDRO) granted [WeRide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WeRide "WeRide"), a global leader in autonomous driving technology, a driverless permit which authorises it to operate autonomously on public roads in the Furttal region. This is the first driverless Robotaxi permit for passengers issued in Switzerland.[\[214\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-228)

### Environment

Main articles: [Environment of Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environment_of_Switzerland "Environment of Switzerland") and [Waste management in Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waste_management_in_Switzerland "Waste management in Switzerland")

Switzerland has one of the best environmental records among developed nations.[\[215\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-229) It is a signatory to the [Kyoto Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kyoto_Protocol "Kyoto Protocol"); alongside Mexico and South Korea, it was a founding member of the [Environmental Integrity Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_Integrity_Group "Environmental Integrity Group") (EIG).[\[216\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-230)

The country is active in recycling and anti-littering programs and is one of the world's top recyclers, recovering 66% to 96% of recyclable materials, varying across the country.[\[217\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-231) The 2014 Global Green Economy Index placed Switzerland among the top 10 green economies.[\[218\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-232)

Switzerland has an economic system for garbage disposal, which is based mostly on recycling and energy-producing [incinerators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incinerator "Incinerator").[\[219\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-233) As in other European countries, the illegal disposal of garbage is heavily fined. In almost all Swiss municipalities, mandatory stickers or dedicated garbage bags allow the identification of disposable garbage.[\[220\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-234)

## Demographics

Main articles: [Demographics of Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Switzerland "Demographics of Switzerland"), [Swiss people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_people "Swiss people"), and [Crime in Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_in_Switzerland "Crime in Switzerland")

Further information: [List of Swiss people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Swiss_people "List of Swiss people")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9f/Bev%C3%B6lkerungsdichte_der_Schweiz_2019.png/500px-Bev%C3%B6lkerungsdichte_der_Schweiz_2019.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bev%C3%B6lkerungsdichte_der_Schweiz_2019.png) Population density in Switzerland (2019)[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/72/Ausl%C3%A4nderanteil_der_Schweiz_2019.png/500px-Ausl%C3%A4nderanteil_der_Schweiz_2019.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Ausl%C3%A4nderanteil_der_Schweiz_2019.png) Percentage of foreigners in Switzerland (2019)

| **Migration status** | **Year** |  | **pct.** | **Change** |
| Without migration background | 2024 |  | 58% | -7% |
| 2012 |  | 65% |
| [Immigrants: First Generation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigrant_generations#First_generation "Immigrant generations") | 2024 |  | 33% | +5% |

Permanent resident population aged 15 or over, by migration status, 2012/2024[\[221\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-235)

The Swiss population is about 9 million as of June 2024.[\[222\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-236) Like other developed countries, Switzerland experienced rapid population growth during the [industrial era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_era "Industrial era"), with the number of inhabitants quadrupling between 1800 and 1990. Population growth is projected to continue to 2035, due mostly to immigration. Like [most of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ageing_of_Europe "Ageing of Europe"), Switzerland faces an [ageing population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ageing_population "Ageing population"), with a fertility rate close to [replacement level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Replacement_fertility_rate "Replacement fertility rate").[\[223\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-237) Switzerland has one of the world's oldest populations, with an average age of 44.5 years.[\[224\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-WorldFactbook-238)

According to the [World Factbook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Factbook "World Factbook"), as of 2020, the largest ethnic group in the country is [Swiss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_people "Swiss people") (69.2%), which includes several ethno-linguistic groups, followed by German (4.2%), Italian (3.2%), Portuguese (2.5%), French (2.1%), Kosovan (1.1%), Turkish (1%), and other (16.7%).[\[224\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-WorldFactbook-238) The [Council of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Europe "Council of Europe") suggests a population of around 30,000 [Romani people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romani_people "Romani people").[\[225\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-239)
