### Immigration

Main article: [Immigration to Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_Switzerland "Immigration to Switzerland")

In 2023, resident foreigners made up 26.3% of Switzerland's population.[\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-20min.ch-26) Most of these (83%) were from European countries. Italy provided the largest single group of foreigners, accounting for 14.7% of total foreign population, followed closely by Germany (14.0%), Portugal (11.7%), France (6.6%), Kosovo (5.1%), Spain (3.9%), Turkey (3.1%), [North Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Macedonia "North Macedonia") (3.1%), Serbia (2.8%), Austria (2.0%), United Kingdom (1.9%), Bosnia and Herzegovina (1.3%) and Croatia (1.3%). Immigrants from [Sri Lanka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Lanka "Sri Lanka") (1.3%), most of them former [Tamil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sri_Lankan_Tamils "Sri Lankan Tamils") refugees, were the largest group of Asian origin (7.9%).[\[227\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-241)

2021 figures show that 39.5% (compared to 34.7% in 2012) of the permanent resident population aged 15 or over (around 2.89million), had an immigrant background. 38% of the population with an immigrant background (1.1million) held Swiss citizenship.[\[228\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-242)[\[229\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-243)

In the 2000s, domestic and international institutions expressed concern about what was perceived as an increase in [xenophobia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xenophobia "Xenophobia"). In reply to one critical report, the Federal Council noted that "racism unfortunately is present in Switzerland", but stated that the high proportion of foreign citizens in the country, as well as the generally successful integration of foreigners, underlined Switzerland's openness.[\[230\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-244) A follow-up study conducted in 2018 reported that 59% considered [racism a serious problem in Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_to_Switzerland#Racism "Immigration to Switzerland").[\[231\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-245) The proportion of the population that claimed to have been targeted by racial discrimination increased from 10% in 2014 to almost 17% in 2018, according to the Federal Statistical Office.[\[232\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-246)

### Languages

Main article: [Languages of Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Switzerland "Languages of Switzerland")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8f/Karte_Schweizer_Sprachgebiete_2026.png/500px-Karte_Schweizer_Sprachgebiete_2026.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Karte_Schweizer_Sprachgebiete_2026.png) National languages in Switzerland (2026):[\[233\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-LangStat-247)

German (62%)

French (22.7%)

Italian (8.2%)

[Romansh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romansh_language "Romansh language") (0.5%)

Switzerland has four [national languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_language "National language"): mainly German (spoken natively by 62% of the population in 2026); French (22.7%) spoken natively in the west; and Italian (8.2%) spoken natively in the south.[\[234\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-248)[\[233\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-LangStat-247) The fourth national language, [Romansh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romansh_language "Romansh language") (0.5%), is a [Romance language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romance_language "Romance language") spoken locally in the southeastern trilingual [canton of Grisons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canton_of_Grisons "Canton of Grisons"), and is designated by Article 4 of the Federal Constitution as a national language along with German, French, and Italian. In Article 70 it is mentioned as an official language if the authorities communicate with persons who speak Romansh. However, federal laws and other official acts do not need to be decreed in Romansh.

In 2016, the languages most spoken at home among permanent residents aged 15 and older were [Swiss German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_German "Swiss German") (59.4%), French (23.5%), [Standard German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_German "Standard German") (10.6%), and Italian (8.5%). Other languages spoken at home included English (5.0%), [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language "Portuguese language") (3.8%), [Albanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albanian_language "Albanian language") (3.0%), Spanish (2.6%) and [Serbian and Croatian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Serbian_and_Croatian "Serbian and Croatian") (2.5%). 6.9% reported speaking another language at home.[\[235\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-249) In 2019 more than two-thirds (68%) of the permanent resident population indicated speaking more than one language regularly.[\[236\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-250)[\[237\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-251)

The federal government is obliged to communicate in the official languages, and in the federal parliament simultaneous translation is provided from and into German, French and Italian.[\[238\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-252)

Aside from the official forms of their respective languages, the four linguistic regions of Switzerland also have local dialectal forms. The role played by dialects in each linguistic region varies dramatically: in German-speaking regions, [Swiss German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_German "Swiss German") dialects have become more prevalent since the second half of the 20th century, especially in the media, and are used as an everyday language for many, while the [Swiss variety of Standard German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Standard_German "Swiss Standard German") is almost always used instead of dialect for written communication (cf. [diglossic usage of a language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diglossia "Diglossia")).[\[239\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-dialhls-253) Conversely, in the French-speaking regions, local [Franco-Provençal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Franco-Proven%C3%A7al "Franco-Provençal") dialects have almost disappeared (only 6.3% of the population of Valais, 3.9% of Fribourg, and 3.1% of Jura still spoke dialects at the end of the 20th century), while in the Italian-speaking regions, the use of [Lombard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lombard_language "Lombard language") dialects is mostly limited to family settings and casual conversation.[\[239\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-dialhls-253)

The principal official languages have terms not used outside of Switzerland, known as [Helvetisms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helvetism "Helvetism"). German Helvetisms are, roughly speaking, a large group of words typical of [Swiss Standard German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Standard_German "Swiss Standard German") that do not appear in [Standard German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_German "Standard German"), nor in other German dialects. These include terms from Switzerland's surrounding language cultures (German _Billett_[\[240\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-254) from French), from similar terms in another language (Italian _azione_ used not only as _act_ but also as _discount_ from German _Aktion_).[\[241\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-CHDuden-255) [Swiss French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_French "Swiss French"), while generally close to the French of France, also contains some Helvetisms. The most frequent characteristics of Helvetisms are in vocabulary, phrases, and pronunciation, although certain Helvetisms denote themselves as special in syntax and [orthography](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orthography "Orthography"). [Duden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duden "Duden"), the comprehensive German dictionary, contains about 3000 Helvetisms.[\[241\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-CHDuden-255) Current French dictionaries, such as the [Petit Larousse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petit_Larousse "Petit Larousse"), include several hundred Helvetisms; notably, Swiss French uses different terms than that of France for the numbers 70 ( _septante_) and 90 ( _nonante_) and often 80 ( _huitante_) as well.[\[242\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-256)

Learning one of the other national languages is compulsory for all Swiss pupils, hence many Swiss are supposed to be at least [bilingual](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bilingual "Bilingual"), especially those belonging to linguistic minority groups.[\[243\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-257) Because the largest part of Switzerland is German-speaking, many French, Italian, and Romansh speakers migrating to the rest of Switzerland and the children of those non-German-speaking Swiss born within the rest of Switzerland speak German. While learning one of the other national languages at school is important, most Swiss learn English to communicate with Swiss speakers of other languages, as it is perceived as a neutral means of communication. English often functions as the _de facto_ [lingua franca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingua_franca "Lingua franca").[\[244\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-258)

### Religion

Main article: [Religion in Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Switzerland "Religion in Switzerland")

Religion in Switzerland (age 15+, 2021–2023):[\[245\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-RelOverview2023-259)[\[o\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-national-survey-260)

1. [Catholicism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Switzerland "Catholic Church in Switzerland") (31.9%)
2. [Swiss Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant_Church_of_Switzerland "Protestant Church of Switzerland") (20.4%)
3. [Unaffiliated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irreligion "Irreligion") (33.8%)
4. [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Switzerland "Islam in Switzerland") (5.80%)
5. Other religions (7.20%)
6. Undetermined (0.90%)

[Christianity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianity "Christianity") is the predominant religion according to national surveys of Swiss Federal Statistical Office,[\[o\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-national-survey-260) divided between the [Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church "Catholic Church") (31.9% of the population), the [Swiss Reformed Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Reformed_Church "Swiss Reformed Church") (20.4%) and other Christian denominations.[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-SFOReligions-6)

Switzerland has no official [state religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_religion "State religion"), though most [cantons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantons_of_Switzerland "Cantons of Switzerland") (except [Geneva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canton_of_Geneva "Canton of Geneva") and [Neuchâtel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canton_of_Neuch%C3%A2tel "Canton of Neuchâtel")) recognise official churches, either the Catholic Church or the Swiss Reformed Church. These churches, and in some cantons the [Old Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Catholic_Church "Old Catholic Church") and Jewish congregations, are financed by official taxation of members.[\[247\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-262) In 2020, the Roman Catholic Church had 3,048,475 registered and [church tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_tax "Church tax") paying members (corresponding to 35.2% of the total population), while the Swiss Reformed Church had 2,015,816 members (23.3% of the total population).[\[248\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-263)[\[p\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-church-membership-264)

As of 2023, according to a national survey conducted by the Swiss Federal Statistical Office,[\[o\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-national-survey-260) the next largest Christian groups are [Eastern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church "Eastern Orthodox Church") and [Oriental Orthodoxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oriental_Orthodox_Churches "Oriental Orthodox Churches") (2.8%), [Reformed Evangelicalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reformed_evangelical "Reformed evangelical") (1.1%), Neo- [Pietism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pietism "Pietism") (0.4%), [Pentecostalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecostalism "Pentecostalism") and other [Charismatic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charismatic_Christianity "Charismatic Christianity") communities (0.4%, mostly incorporated in the [Schweizer Pfingstmission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schweizer_Pfingstmission "Schweizer Pfingstmission")), [Adventism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adventism "Adventism") (0.4%), [Apostolic communities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Apostolic_Church "New Apostolic Church") (0.2%), the [Old Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_Catholic_Church "Old Catholic Church") (0.1%), and other Christian denominations (0.3%).

Non-Christian religions are [Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Switzerland "Islam in Switzerland") (5.8%), [Hinduism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hinduism "Hinduism") (0.6%), [Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism "Buddhism") (0.5%), [Judaism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Judaism "Judaism") (0.2%) and others (0.2%).[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-SFOReligions-6)[\[o\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-national-survey-260)

Close to one-third (33.8%) of the population are not affiliated with a religious community.[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-SFOReligions-6) Some traditionally Protestant cantons and cities have a slight Catholic majority, reflecting a growing trend in seculairsation and irreligion since 1970; as 2012, the proportion of religiously unaffiliated people was largest in Basel-City (42%), canton of Neuchâtel (38%), canton of Geneva (35%), canton of Vaud (26%), or Zurich city (city: >25%; canton: 23%).[\[249\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-265)

By 2024, half the Swiss population never attended a religious event in the 12 months preceding, according to the Federal Statistical Office. More than a quarter who left their religion cite loss of faith or disagreement with their religious community.[\[250\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-p541-266)

### Education

Main article: [Education in Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Switzerland "Education in Switzerland")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/61/11-11-24-basel-by-ralfr-035.jpg/500px-11-11-24-basel-by-ralfr-035.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:11-11-24-basel-by-ralfr-035.jpg) The [University of Basel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Basel "University of Basel") is Switzerland's oldest university (1460).[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/63/Swiss_scientists.jpg/500px-Swiss_scientists.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Swiss_scientists.jpg) Some Swiss scientists who played a key role in their discipline (clockwise):

[Leonhard Euler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonhard_Euler "Leonhard Euler") (mathematics)

[Louis Agassiz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_Agassiz "Louis Agassiz") (glaciology)

[Auguste Piccard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auguste_Piccard "Auguste Piccard") (aeronautics)

[Albert Einstein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Albert_Einstein "Albert Einstein") (physics)

Education in Switzerland is diverse, because the [constitution of Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Switzerland "Constitution of Switzerland") delegates the operation for the school system to the [cantons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canton_of_Switzerland "Canton of Switzerland").[\[251\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Education-267) Public and private schools are available, including many private international schools.

#### Primary education

The minimum age for primary school is about six years, but most cantons provide a free "children's school" starting at age four or five.[\[251\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Education-267) Primary school continues until grade four, five or six, depending on the school. Traditionally, the first foreign language in school was one of the other Swiss languages, although in 2000, English was elevated in a few cantons.[\[251\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Education-267) At the end of primary school or at the beginning of secondary school, pupils are assigned according to their capacities into one of several sections (often three). The fastest learners are taught advanced classes to prepare for further studies and the [matura](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matura "Matura"),[\[251\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Education-267) while other students receive an education adapted to their needs.

#### Tertiary education

Switzerland hosts [12 universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_universities_in_Switzerland "List of universities in Switzerland"), ten of which are maintained at [cantonal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantonal "Cantonal") level and usually offer non-technical subjects. It ranked 87th on the 2019 [Academic Ranking of World Universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_Ranking_of_World_Universities "Academic Ranking of World Universities").[\[252\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-268) The largest is the [University of Zurich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Zurich "University of Zurich") with nearly 25,000 students.[\[253\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-269) The [Swiss Federal Institute of Technology Zurich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Federal_Institute_of_Technology_Zurich "Swiss Federal Institute of Technology Zurich") (ETHZ) and the University of Zurich are listed 20th and 54th respectively, on the 2015 Academic Ranking of World Universities.[\[254\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-270)

The federal government sponsors two institutes: the [Swiss Federal Institute of Technology Zurich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Federal_Institute_of_Technology_Zurich "Swiss Federal Institute of Technology Zurich") (ETHZ) in [Zurich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zurich "Zurich"), founded in 1855 and the [École Polytechnique Fédérale de Lausanne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89cole_Polytechnique_F%C3%A9d%C3%A9rale_de_Lausanne "École Polytechnique Fédérale de Lausanne") (EPFL) in [Lausanne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lausanne "Lausanne"), founded in 1969, formerly associated with the [University of Lausanne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Lausanne "University of Lausanne").[\[q\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-ETH_ranking-271)[\[255\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-272)[\[256\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-273)

Eight of the world's ten best hotel schools are located in Switzerland.[\[257\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-274) In addition, various [universities of applied sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universities_of_Applied_Sciences "Universities of Applied Sciences") are available. In business and management studies, the [University of St. Gallen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_St._Gallen "University of St. Gallen"), (HSG) is ranked 436th in the world according to [QS World University Rankings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/QS_World_University_Rankings "QS World University Rankings")[\[258\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-275) and the [International Institute for Management Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Institute_for_Management_Development "International Institute for Management Development") (IMD), was ranked first in open programmes worldwide _._[\[259\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-276) Switzerland has the second highest rate (almost 18% in 2003) of foreign students in tertiary education, after Australia (slightly over 18%).[\[260\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-277)[\[261\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-278)

The [Graduate Institute of International and Development Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graduate_Institute_of_International_and_Development_Studies "Graduate Institute of International and Development Studies"), located in [Geneva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva "Geneva"), is continental Europe's oldest graduate school of international and development studies. It is widely held to be one of its most prestigious.[\[262\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-279)[\[263\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-280)

### Health

Main articles: [Health in Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Switzerland "Health in Switzerland") and [Healthcare in Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_Switzerland "Healthcare in Switzerland")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Life_expectancy_by_WBG_-Switzerland.png/500px-Life_expectancy_by_WBG_-Switzerland.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Life_expectancy_by_WBG_-Switzerland.png) Life expectancy at birth in Switzerland

In the 2023 [OECD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OECD "OECD") "Health at a Glance" report, Switzerland's health statistics indicate several advantages relative to the OECD averages. The country records a [life expectancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Life_expectancy "Life expectancy") of 83.9 years, which is 3.6 years higher than the OECD average. Its preventable [mortality rate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mortality_rate "Mortality rate") is 94 per 100,000 people, which is below the OECD average of 158 per 100,000. The treatable mortality rate in Switzerland, at 39 per 100,000, is also below the OECD average of 79 per 100,000. Additionally, 3.9% of the Swiss population reports their health as bad or very bad, less than the OECD average of 7.9%. The prevalence of [diabetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diabetes "Diabetes") in Switzerland is lower than the OECD average. Switzerland performs better than the OECD benchmarks on 95% of the health indicators analysed.[\[264\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-281)

A new measure of expected [human capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_capital "Human capital") calculated for 195 countries from 1990 to 2016 and defined for each birth cohort as the expected years lived from age 20 to 64 years and adjusted for educational attainment, learning or education quality, and functional health status was published by _[The Lancet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Lancet "The Lancet")_ in September 2018. Switzerland had the twelfth highest level of expected human capital with 25 health, education, and learning-adjusted expected years lived between age 20 and 64 years.[\[265\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-282) According to a study conducted by Swiss insurance company [CSS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CSS "CSS") in 2023, about one third of the Swiss "feel unhealthy or ill".[\[266\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-SWI_swissinfo.ch_2023_f223-283)

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Switzerland "Culture of Switzerland")

See also: [Music of Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Switzerland "Music of Switzerland"), [Swiss folklore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_folklore "Swiss folklore"), and [Alpine culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpine_culture "Alpine culture")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/dd/Vals06.JPG/500px-Vals06.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Vals06.JPG) [Alphorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alphorn "Alphorn") concert in [Vals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vals,_Switzerland "Vals, Switzerland")

Swiss culture is characterised by diversity, which is reflected in diverse traditional customs.[\[267\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-FDFA-Culture-284) A region may be in some ways culturally connected to the neighbouring country that shares its language, all rooted in western [European culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_culture "European culture").[\[268\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-285) The linguistically isolated [Romansh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romansh_language "Romansh language") culture in [Graubünden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graub%C3%BCnden "Graubünden") in eastern Switzerland constitutes an exception. It survives only in the upper valleys of the Rhine and the Inn and strives to maintain its rare linguistic tradition.

Switzerland is home to notable contributors to art, architecture, [literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_literature "Swiss literature"), [music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Switzerland "Music of Switzerland") and sciences. In addition, the country attracted creatives during times of unrest or war.[\[269\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-286) Some [1000 museums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_museums_in_Switzerland "List of museums in Switzerland") are found in the country.[\[267\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-FDFA-Culture-284)

Among the most important cultural performances held annually are the [Paléo Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pal%C3%A9o_Festival "Paléo Festival"), [Lucerne Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucerne_Festival "Lucerne Festival"),[\[270\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-287) the [Montreux Jazz Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Montreux_Jazz_Festival "Montreux Jazz Festival"),[\[271\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-288) the [Locarno International Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Locarno_International_Film_Festival "Locarno International Film Festival") and [Art Basel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Basel "Art Basel").[\[272\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-289) In addition, Switzerland has hosted the [Eurovision Song Contest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurovision_Song_Contest "Eurovision Song Contest") thrice, in 1956 (the inaugural edition, held in Lugano), 1989 (Lausanne), and 2025 (Basel), and won three times, through [Lys Assia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lys_Assia "Lys Assia") in 1956, [Celine Dion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celine_Dion "Celine Dion") in 1988, and [Nemo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nemo_(singer) "Nemo (singer)") in 2024.

Alpine symbolism played an essential role in shaping Swiss history and the Swiss national identity.[\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Zimmer-2004-28)[\[273\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-290) Many alpine areas and [ski resorts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ski_resort "Ski resort") attract visitors for [winter sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winter_sports "Winter sports") as well as hiking and [mountain biking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_biking "Mountain biking") in summer. The quieter seasons are spring and autumn. A traditional pastoral culture predominates in many areas, and small farms are omnipresent in rural areas. Folk art is nurtured in organisations across the country. Switzerland most directly in appears in music, dance, poetry, wood carving, and embroidery. The [alphorn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alphorn "Alphorn"), a trumpet-like musical instrument made of wood has joined [yodelling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yodel "Yodel") and the accordion as epitomes of traditional [Swiss music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_music "Swiss music").[\[274\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-291)[\[275\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-292)

### Literature

Main article: [Swiss literature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_literature "Swiss literature")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/Jean-Jacques_Rousseau_%28painted_portrait%29.jpg/500px-Jean-Jacques_Rousseau_%28painted_portrait%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Jean-Jacques_Rousseau_(painted_portrait).jpg) [Jean-Jacques Rousseau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Jacques_Rousseau "Jean-Jacques Rousseau") was not only a writer but also an influential philosopher of the eighteenth century.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

The earliest forms of literature were in German, reflecting the language's early predominance. In the 18th century, French became fashionable in Bern and elsewhere, while the influence of the French-speaking allies and subject lands increased.[\[276\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-293)

Among the classic authors of Swiss literature are [Jeremias Gotthelf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeremias_Gotthelf "Jeremias Gotthelf") (1797–1854) and [Gottfried Keller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gottfried_Keller "Gottfried Keller") (1819–1890); later writers are [Max Frisch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Frisch "Max Frisch") (1911–1991) and [Friedrich Dürrenmatt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friedrich_D%C3%BCrrenmatt "Friedrich Dürrenmatt") (1921–1990), whose _Das Versprechen_ ( _[The Pledge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Pledge:_Requiem_for_the_Detective_Novel "The Pledge: Requiem for the Detective Novel")_) was released as a Hollywood film in 2001, starring Jack Nicholson.[\[277\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Literature-294)

Famous French-speaking writers were [Jean-Jacques Rousseau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jean-Jacques_Rousseau "Jean-Jacques Rousseau") (1712–1778) and [Germaine de Staël](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Germaine_de_Sta%C3%ABl "Germaine de Staël") (1766–1817). More recent authors include [Charles Ferdinand Ramuz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charles_Ferdinand_Ramuz "Charles Ferdinand Ramuz") (1878–1947), whose novels describe the lives of peasants and mountain dwellers, set in a harsh environment, and [Blaise Cendrars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blaise_Cendrars "Blaise Cendrars") (born Frédéric Sauser, 1887–1961).[\[277\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Literature-294) Italian and Romansh-speaking authors also contributed to the Swiss literary landscape, generally in proportion to their number.

Probably the most famous Swiss literary creation, _[Heidi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heidi "Heidi")_, the story of an orphan girl who lives with her grandfather in the Alps, is one of the most popular children's books and has come to be a symbol of Switzerland. Her creator, [Johanna Spyri](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johanna_Spyri "Johanna Spyri") (1827–1901), wrote a number of books on similar themes.[\[277\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Literature-294)

### Media

Main article: [Media of Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_of_Switzerland "Media of Switzerland")

|     |     |
| --- | --- |
| [![icon](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/9/99/Question_book-new.svg/120px-Question_book-new.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Question_book-new.svg) | This section **needs [more citations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability "Wikipedia:Verifiability")**. Please help [improve this section](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:EditPage/Switzerland "Special:EditPage/Switzerland") by [adding citations to reliable sources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners "Help:Referencing for beginners"). Unsourced material may be challenged and [removed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability#Burden_of_evidence "Wikipedia:Verifiability")._(February 2024)__([Learn how and when to remove this message](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal "Help:Maintenance template removal"))_ |

[Freedom of the press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_of_the_press "Freedom of the press") and the right to [free expression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_expression "Free expression") is guaranteed in the constitution.[\[278\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Media-295) The [Swiss News Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schweizerische_Depeschenagentur "Schweizerische Depeschenagentur") (SNA) broadcasts information in three of the four national languages—on politics, economics, society and culture. The SNA supplies almost all Swiss media and foreign media with its reporting.[\[278\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-Media-295)

In Switzerland, the most influential newspapers include the German-language _[Tages-Anzeiger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tages-Anzeiger "Tages-Anzeiger")_ and _[Neue Zürcher Zeitung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neue_Z%C3%BCrcher_Zeitung "Neue Zürcher Zeitung")_, as well as the French-language _[Le Temps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Temps "Le Temps")_. Additionally, almost every city has at least one local newspaper published in the predominant local language.[\[279\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-296)[\[280\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-297)

The government exerts greater control over broadcast media than print media, especially due to financing and licensing.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\] The Swiss Broadcasting Corporation, an [EBU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EBU "EBU") member, whose name was recently changed to [SRG SSR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SRG_SSR "SRG SSR"), is charged with the production and distribution of radio and television content. SRG SSR studios are distributed across the various language regions. Radio content is produced in six central and four regional studios while video media are produced in [Geneva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva "Geneva"), [Zurich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zurich "Zurich"), [Basel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basel "Basel"), and [Lugano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lugano "Lugano"). An extensive cable network allows most Swiss to access content from neighbouring countries.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\]

### Cuisine

Main article: [Swiss cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_cuisine "Swiss cuisine")

See also: [Culinary Heritage of Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culinary_Heritage_of_Switzerland "Culinary Heritage of Switzerland")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Full_cheese_fondue_set_-_in_Switzerland.JPG/500px-Full_cheese_fondue_set_-_in_Switzerland.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Full_cheese_fondue_set_-_in_Switzerland.JPG) [Fondue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fondue "Fondue") is melted cheese, into which bread is dipped.

The cuisine is multifaceted. While dishes such as [fondue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fondue "Fondue"), [raclette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raclette "Raclette") or [rösti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C3%B6sti "Rösti") are omnipresent, each region developed its gastronomy according to the varieties of climate and language, for example, _[Zürcher Geschnetzeltes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Z%C3%BCrcher_Geschnetzeltes "Zürcher Geschnetzeltes")_, engl.: sliced meat Zurich style.[\[281\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-298) Traditional Swiss cuisine uses ingredients similar to those in other European countries, as well as unique [dairy products](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dairy_product "Dairy product") and cheeses such as [Gruyère](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gruy%C3%A8re "Gruyère") or [Emmental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmental_(cheese) "Emmental (cheese)"), produced in the valleys of [Gruyères](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gruy%C3%A8res "Gruyères") and [Emmental](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmental "Emmental"). The number of fine-dining establishments is high, particularly in western Switzerland.[\[282\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-299)[\[283\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-300)

[Chocolate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_chocolate "Swiss chocolate") has been made in Switzerland since the 18th century. Its reputation grew at the end of the 19th century with the invention of modern techniques such as [conching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conching "Conching") and [tempering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tempering_chocolate "Tempering chocolate"), which enabled higher quality. Another breakthrough was the invention of solid milk chocolate in 1875 by [Daniel Peter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Peter "Daniel Peter"). The Swiss are the world's largest chocolate consumers.[\[284\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-301)[\[285\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-302)

The most popular alcoholic drink is wine. Switzerland is notable for its variety of grape varieties, reflecting the large variations in [terroirs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terroir "Terroir"). [Swiss wine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_wine "Swiss wine") is produced mainly in [Valais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Valais "Valais"), [Vaud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaud "Vaud") ( [Lavaux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lavaux "Lavaux")), [Geneva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geneva_(wine_region) "Geneva (wine region)") and Ticino, with a small majority of white wines. Vineyards have been cultivated in Switzerland since the Roman era, even though traces of a more ancient origin can be found. The most widespread varieties are the [Chasselas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chasselas "Chasselas") (called [Fendant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fendant "Fendant") in Valais) and [Pinot Noir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pinot_Noir "Pinot Noir"). [Merlot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merlot "Merlot") is the main variety produced in Ticino.[\[286\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-303)[\[287\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-304) Eighty-two percent of the population regularly [drinks alcoholic beverages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_in_Switzerland#Alcoholism "Health in Switzerland").[\[288\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-How_healthy_are_the_Swiss-305)

### Sports

Main article: [Sport in Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Switzerland "Sport in Switzerland")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/46/Allalinhorn_4027_2007_04_17.JPG/500px-Allalinhorn_4027_2007_04_17.JPG?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Allalinhorn_4027_2007_04_17.JPG) Ski area over the glaciers of [Saas-Fee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saas-Fee "Saas-Fee")

[Skiing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skiing "Skiing"), [snowboarding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snowboarding "Snowboarding") and [mountaineering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountaineering "Mountaineering") are among the most popular sports, reflecting the nature of the country.[\[289\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-306) Winter sports are practised by natives and visitors. The [bobsleigh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bobsleigh "Bobsleigh") was invented in [St. Moritz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Moritz "St. Moritz").[\[290\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-307) The first [world ski championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FIS_Alpine_World_Ski_Championships "FIS Alpine World Ski Championships") were held in [Mürren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%BCrren "Mürren") (1931) and St. Moritz (1934). The latter town hosted the [second Winter Olympic Games](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1928_Winter_Olympics "1928 Winter Olympics") in 1928 and the [fifth edition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1948_Winter_Olympics "1948 Winter Olympics") in 1948. Among its most successful skiers and world champions are [Pirmin Zurbriggen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pirmin_Zurbriggen "Pirmin Zurbriggen") and [Didier Cuche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Didier_Cuche "Didier Cuche").

The most prominently watched sports in Switzerland are [football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Football_in_Switzerland "Football in Switzerland") and [ice hockey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schweizerischer_Eishockeyverband "Schweizerischer Eishockeyverband").[\[291\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-308)

The headquarters of the international football's and ice hockey's governing bodies, the [International Federation of Association Football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Federation_of_Association_Football "International Federation of Association Football") (FIFA) and [International Ice Hockey Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Ice_Hockey_Federation "International Ice Hockey Federation") (IIHF) are located in Zurich. Many other headquarters of international sports federations are located in Switzerland. For example, the [International Olympic Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Olympic_Committee "International Olympic Committee") (IOC), IOC's [Olympic Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympic_Museum "Olympic Museum") and the [Court of Arbitration for Sport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Court_of_Arbitration_for_Sport "Court of Arbitration for Sport") (CAS) are located in [Lausanne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lausanne "Lausanne").

Switzerland hosted the [1954 FIFA World Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1954_FIFA_World_Cup "1954 FIFA World Cup") and was the joint host, with Austria, of the [UEFA Euro 2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UEFA_Euro_2008 "UEFA Euro 2008") tournament. The [Swiss Super League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swiss_Super_League "Swiss Super League") is the nation's professional football club league, with clubs including [BSC Young Boys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BSC_Young_Boys "BSC Young Boys") performing consistently in European club competitions. Europe's highest football pitch, at 2,000 metres (6,600ft) above sea level, is located in Switzerland, the _Ottmar Hitzfeld Stadium_.[\[292\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-309)

Many Swiss follow [ice hockey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_hockey "Ice hockey") and support one of the 14 teams of the [National League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_League_(ice_hockey) "National League (ice hockey)"), which is the most attended league in Europe.[\[293\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-310) In 2009, Switzerland hosted the [IIHF World Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_IIHF_World_Championship "2009 IIHF World Championship") for the tenth time.[\[294\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-311) It also became [World Vice-Champion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2013_IIHF_World_Championship "2013 IIHF World Championship") in 2013, 2018 and 2024. Its numerous lakes make Switzerland an attractive sailing destination. The largest, [Lake Geneva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Geneva "Lake Geneva"), is the home of the sailing team [Alinghi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alinghi "Alinghi") which was the first European team to win the [America's Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/America's_Cup "America's Cup") in 2003 and which successfully defended the title in 2007.

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/Roger_Federer_%2826_June_2009%2C_Wimbledon%29_2_new.jpg/500px-Roger_Federer_%2826_June_2009%2C_Wimbledon%29_2_new.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Roger_Federer_(26_June_2009,_Wimbledon)_2_new.jpg) [Roger Federer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Federer "Roger Federer") has won 20 Grand Slam singles titles, making him among the most successful men's tennis players ever.[\[295\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-312)

Swiss tennis player [Roger Federer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roger_Federer "Roger Federer") is widely regarded as among the sport's greatest players. He won 20 [Grand Slam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Slam_(tennis) "Grand Slam (tennis)") tournaments overall including a record 8 [Wimbledon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Championships,_Wimbledon "The Championships, Wimbledon") titles. He won six [ATP Finals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ATP_Finals "ATP Finals").[\[296\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-313) He was ranked no. 1 in the [ATP rankings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ATP_rankings "ATP rankings") for a record 237 consecutive weeks. He ended 2004, 2005, 2006, 2007 and [2009](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_ATP_World_Tour "2009 ATP World Tour") ranked no. 1. Fellow Swiss players [Martina Hingis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Martina_Hingis "Martina Hingis") and [Stan Wawrinka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stan_Wawrinka "Stan Wawrinka") also won multiple Grand Slam titles. Switzerland won the [Davis Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Davis_Cup "Davis Cup") title in 2014.

[Motorsport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motorsport "Motorsport") racecourses and events were banned in Switzerland following the [1955 Le Mans disaster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1955_Le_Mans_disaster "1955 Le Mans disaster") with exceptions for events such as [hillclimbing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillclimbing "Hillclimbing"). The country continued to produce successful racing drivers such as [Clay Regazzoni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clay_Regazzoni "Clay Regazzoni"), [Sébastien Buemi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S%C3%A9bastien_Buemi "Sébastien Buemi"), [Jo Siffert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jo_Siffert "Jo Siffert"), [Dominique Aegerter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominique_Aegerter "Dominique Aegerter"), successful [World Touring Car Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Touring_Car_Championship "World Touring Car Championship") driver [Alain Menu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alain_Menu "Alain Menu"), [2014 24 Hours of Le Mans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_24_Hours_of_Le_Mans "2014 24 Hours of Le Mans") winner [Marcel Fässler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marcel_F%C3%A4ssler_(racing_driver) "Marcel Fässler (racing driver)") and 2015 [24 Hours Nürburgring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/24_Hours_N%C3%BCrburgring "24 Hours Nürburgring") winner [Nico Müller](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nico_M%C3%BCller "Nico Müller"). [Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A1_Team_Switzerland "A1 Team Switzerland") also won the [A1GP World Cup of Motorsport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/A1_Grand_Prix "A1 Grand Prix") in [2007–08](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2007%E2%80%9308_A1_Grand_Prix_season "2007–08 A1 Grand Prix season") with driver [Neel Jani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neel_Jani "Neel Jani"). Swiss [motorcycle racer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motorcycle_racer "Motorcycle racer") [Thomas Lüthi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_L%C3%BCthi "Thomas Lüthi") won the 2005 [MotoGP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MotoGP "MotoGP") World Championship in the 125cc category. The ban was ultimately lifted in May 2022, on the grounds that safety in motorsport had vastly improved since the ban was first imposed. However, despite the ban being lifted in 2022, Motorsport will officially be legalised on June 1, 2026, meaning Motorsport will be legal for the first time in 71 years.[\[297\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-314)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/Turnerundsennenschwinger.jpg/500px-Turnerundsennenschwinger.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Turnerundsennenschwinger.jpg) Traditional wrestling

Traditional sports include Swiss wrestling or _Schwingen_, a tradition from the rural central cantons and considered the national sport by some. [Hornussen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hornussen "Hornussen") is another indigenous Swiss sport, which is like a cross between baseball and golf.[\[298\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-315)_[Steinstossen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steinstossen "Steinstossen")_ is the Swiss variant of [stone put](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stone_put "Stone put"), a competition in throwing a heavy stone. Practised only among the alpine population since [prehistoric times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistoric_times "Prehistoric times"), it is recorded to have taken place in [Basel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basel "Basel") in the 13th century. It is central to the [Unspunnenfest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unspunnenfest "Unspunnenfest"), first held in 1805, with its symbol the 83.5 stone named _Unspunnenstein_.[\[299\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-316)

## Notes

01. [1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_ref-fedcit_2-0) [2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_ref-fedcit_2-1)Bern is referred to as "federal city" (German: _Bundesstadt_; French: _ville fédérale_; Italian: _città federale_; Romansh: _citad federala_). Swiss law does not designate a _capital_ as such, but the federal parliament and government are installed in Bern, while other federal institutions, such as the federal courts, are in other cities ( [Bellinzona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bellinzona "Bellinzona"), [Lausanne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lausanne "Lausanne"), [Lucerne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucerne "Lucerne"), [Neuchâtel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neuch%C3%A2tel "Neuchâtel"), [St. Gallen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/St._Gallen "St. Gallen") a.o.).
02. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_ref-r%C3%BCtlischwur_9-0)Traditional date. The original date of the [Rütli Oath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/R%C3%BCtli_Oath "Rütli Oath") was 1307 (reported by [Aegidius Tschudi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aegidius_Tschudi "Aegidius Tschudi") in the 16th century) and is just one among several comparable treaties between more or less the same parties during that period. The date of the [Federal Charter of 1291](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Charter_of_1291 "Federal Charter of 1291") was selected in 1891 for the official celebration of the "Confederacy's 600th anniversary".
03. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_ref-federal_constitution_10-0)A solemn declaration of the _[Tagsatzung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagsatzung "Tagsatzung")_ declared the Federal Constitution adopted on 12 September 1848. A resolution of the _Tagsatzung_ of 14 September 1848 specified that the powers of the institutions provided for by the 1815 Federal Treaty would expire at the time of the constitution of the [Federal Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Council_(Switzerland) "Federal Council (Switzerland)"), which took place on 16 November 1848.
04. 05. 06. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_ref-CH_definition_21-0)There are several definitions. See [Geography of Switzerland#Western or Central Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Switzerland#Western_or_Central_Europe "Geography of Switzerland").
07. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_ref-24)The canton of [Ticino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ticino "Ticino") is sometimes included as part of Southern Europe, as it lies geographically on the south side of the Alps ( [Po Basin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Po_(river) "Po (river)")).[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-22)[\[17\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-23)
08. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_ref-The_Centre_121-0)Formerly the Christian Democratic People's Party (CVP/PCD), which merged with the BDP party to form the Centre party in 2019
09. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_ref-BDP_122-0)The [Conservative Democratic Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservative_Democratic_Party_of_Switzerland "Conservative Democratic Party of Switzerland") held both SVP seats from 2003 to 2008, and continued to hold one SVP seat until 2015.
10. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_ref-popular_rights_125-0)German: _Volksrechte_; French: _droits populaires_; [Italian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Italian_language "Italian language"): _diritti popolari_
11. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_ref-majority_cantonal_votes_128-0)Each of the six traditional [half-cantons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Half-canton "Half-canton") gets half a vote, and the other 20 cantons get one full vote, adding up to 23 cantonal votes. Each vote or half-vote is selected [based on the votes of their citizens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Popular_vote "Popular vote").
12. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_ref-initiative_1999_129-0)Since 1999, an initiative can also be in the form of a general proposal to be elaborated by Parliament. Still, because it is considered less attractive for various reasons, this initiative has yet to be used.
13. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_ref-Rothschilds_175-0)Assumption made in the study: one third of the shares is "not allocable" and has been distributed equally among current regions.
14. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_ref-nobel_prize_206-0)Nobel prizes in [non-science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-science "Non-science") categories included
15. [1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_ref-national-survey_260-0) [2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_ref-national-survey_260-1) [3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_ref-national-survey_260-2) [4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_ref-national-survey_260-3)Since 2010, statistics of religious affiliation in Switzerland provided by the Swiss Federal Statistical Office are based on a national structural survey of 200,000 people aged 15 years and older (corresponding to 2.5% of the total resident population). Data are [extrapolated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extrapolated "Extrapolated") to obtain statistical results for the whole population (aged 15 years and older). These results are estimates subject to some degree of uncertainty indicated by a [confidence interval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confidence_interval "Confidence interval"), but by merging samples (pooling) from several years it is possible to get more accurate results, including total number of Protestants and information about minority religions. Note: The figures of the structural survey are not entirely comparable to data collection before 2010 based on census figures (counting every person living in Switzerland) or to annual official numbers of church members.[\[246\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_note-261)
16. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_ref-church-membership_264-0)Precise statistics about the membership of churches among the total population in Switzerland is only available for officially registered and [church tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_tax "Church tax") paying members of the [Catholic Church in Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_Switzerland "Catholic Church in Switzerland") and the [Protestant Church of Switzerland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestant_Church_of_Switzerland "Protestant Church of Switzerland") ( _[Landeskirchen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landeskirche "Landeskirche")_).
17. [↑](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Switzerland#cite_ref-ETH_ranking_271-0)In 2008, the ETH Zurich was ranked 15th in the field _Natural Sciences and Mathematics_ by the [Shanghai Academic Ranking of World Universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academic_Ranking_of_World_Universities "Academic Ranking of World Universities") and the EPFL in Lausanne was ranked 18th in the field _Engineering/Technology and Computer Sciences_ by the same ranking.

Als 1848 ein politisch-administratives Zentrum für den neuen Bundesstaat zu bestimmen war, verzichteten die Verfassungsväter darauf, eine Hauptstadt der Schweiz zu bezeichnen und formulierten stattdessen in Artikel 108: "Alles, was sich auf den Sitz der Bundesbehörden bezieht, ist Gegenstand der Bundesgesetzgebung." Die Bundesstadt ist also nicht mehr und nicht weniger als der Sitz der Bundesbehörden.\[In 1848, when a political and administrative centre was being determined for the new federation, the founders of the constitution abstained from designating a capital city for Switzerland and instead formulated in Article 108: "Everything, which relates to seat of the authorities, is the subject of the federal legislation." The federal city is therefore no more and no less than the seat of the federal authorities.\]

The canton of the Ticino marks the geographic descent from high Alps to plain and is, therefore, a land of climatic as well as linguistic transition, where heat and abundant moisture favor almonds, figs, and all the fruits common to southern Europe, except the olive.

Published electronically (1998–) and in print (2002–) simultaneously in three of the national languages of Switzerland: [DHS/HLS/DSS](http://www.hls-dhs-dss.ch/) ( [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20061205103158/http://hls-dhs-dss.ch/) 5 December 2006 at the [Wayback Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayback_Machine "Wayback Machine")): online edition in German, French and Italian.
